  Instance-dependent certification complexity in branch-and-bound



                                                  Abstract
          We study the size of an optimality certificate for a fixed relaxation and specified branch-
      and-bound operations. For continuous problems with a quadratic relaxation gap that vanishes
      at box vertices, we compare minimum cover, partition, and tree certificates with a running
      supremum of near-optimal covering numbers, up to a logarithmic factor. For unconstrained
      objectives with Lipschitz gradients and matching vertex-vanishing upper error, integrals over all
      positive-dimensional root-box faces characterize these sizes within constant factors. Analytic
      sublevel asymptotics then give polynomial and logarithmic rates, including rates governed by
      boundary faces. We also analyze McCormick exact faces, relaxed constraints, objective-cutoff
      propagation, and branching decisions. A fixed path family separates spatial and decomposition
      certificate sizes exponentially in dimension while preserving the local relaxation; one-dimensional
      relaxation-minimizer branching is 4-competitive under an exact-gap oracle. For integer problems,
      a convex-class covering number gives a relaxation-dependent lower bound and an exact semantic
      certificate size. Gaussian sparse regression exhibits linear certificates below root exactness.
      Gaussian binary least squares has matching certificate-size laws at divergent sublinear signal-to-
      noise ratio and distinct thresholds for a sufficient linear-tree criterion and equality of box and
      orthant relaxations. A finite nonnegative least-squares coefficient bound proves the simultaneous
      criterion for square and tall systems. We distinguish certificate size, search nodes, relaxation
      evaluations, and propagation rounds throughout; archived computations illustrate the mechanisms
      and their practical limits.

1     Introduction
Branch-and-bound produces a feasible solution and a proof of its quality. The proof excludes every
region that might contain a substantially better solution, using relaxation bounds, disjunctions,
and domain reductions. Its size can vary greatly between instances of the same dimension. A large
search tree can reflect a weak relaxation, poorly chosen splits, expensive propagation, or difficulty
finding a good incumbent. These causes require different explanations.
    This paper studies the size of the proof for a fixed instance, relaxation, and class of permitted
operations. We call this size the certification complexity. The central question is how the relaxation’s
error interacts with the instance’s near-optimal points. We develop geometric characterizations
for continuous box relaxations, combinatorial lower bounds for convex integer relaxations, and
probabilistic laws in two Gaussian models. We then examine how propagation, branching, and
decomposition change the certificates that can be constructed.

1.1   Certificate size and search work
Let f∗ be the global minimum and let ϵ > 0 be an additive optimality tolerance. A lower-bound
certificate covers the points that must be considered by regions on which a specified test proves that
the objective is at least f∗ − ϵ. A finished branch-and-bound tree supplies such a certificate when
its incumbent is optimal. With any feasible incumbent of value U ≥ f∗ , pruning at L(B) ≥ U − ϵ
still implies L(B) ≥ f∗ − ϵ, so the same lower bounds apply. Constructive upper bounds usually fix
an optimal incumbent; the relevant exceptions state an explicit search convention.
     For spatial certificates, three minima are useful: Ncover counts a smallest cover by valid boxes,
Nrect a smallest rectangular partition, and Ntree the leaves of a smallest tree formed by coordinate
cuts. They satisfy
                                         Ncover ≤ Nrect ≤ Ntree .



                                                   4
These finite minima need not be equal. A lower bound for covers and an upper bound realized by a
tree can nevertheless identify their common asymptotic order. A complete binary tree with L leaves
has 2L − 1 nodes.
     Domain reduction requires additional care. A closed discarded slab can contain a retained
feasible face, whose points the reduction did not exclude. We therefore pair each closed test box
with the subset of points it owns; retained boundaries continue into the surviving region. These
owned families need not be ordinary covers by boxes valid on their whole closures. The geometric
lower bounds apply to them directly through per-test estimates. Section 2 gives the ownership
construction and charges removed pieces, probe tests used in stopping bounds, and relaxation
evaluations (lemma 2.3). Lower bounds after reduction require the stated pointwise gap assumptions.
A weaker bound-gap assumption suffices for raw certificates without reduction.
     We distinguish four quantities:
   (i) the size of a smallest certificate in the chosen class;
  (ii) the number of nodes processed while finding one;
 (iii) the work needed to check its regions, including relaxation evaluations, tightening rounds, and
       propagation rounds;
 (iv) the work needed to find an incumbent of the required quality.
The branching results compare item (ii) with item (i). Propagation and probing can shift work
from nodes to item (iii). A geometric lower bound on owned events becomes a node lower bound
only when the relevant rounds per node are bounded and the model accounts for probe tests. Every
result fixes its relaxation and operation class; a stronger relaxation or an additional operation must
satisfy the hypotheses anew.

1.2   Main results
The continuous results concern ϵ ↓ 0 for a fixed instance. Write D ⊂ Rn for the compact root box,
F ⊆ D for its feasible set, m = f − f∗ , and E(η) = {x ∈ F : m(x) ≤ η}. Constants may depend on
the instance, relaxation, and dimension. The random-model results instead let the problem size
grow and state their probability and scaling assumptions separately.
                                              Q

Suppose the bound satisfies L(B) ≤ f (x) − αqB (x) at every feasible x ∈ B, for a fixed α > 0. A
valid box then obeys αqB (x) ≤ m(x) + ϵ. Each summand of qB is at least the squared distance
p the nearer endpoint, so every point of E(η) certified by that box lies within sup-norm distance
to
   (ϵ + η)/α of a box vertex. This gives the covering lower bound that drives the continuous theory.
     Let N∞ (S, r) count the least number of axis-aligned cubes of side r covering S, and define

                                       η≥0

Under second-order upper error in both objective and constraint violation, a local violation-to-
distance error bound near the full feasible set, and a Lipschitz objective, dyadic subdivision yields

                  cΦα (ϵ) ≤ Ncover ≤ Nrect ≤ Ntree ≤ Tbis ≤ C0 + C log(1/ϵ)Φα (ϵ)                 (2)

for sufficiently small ϵ (theorem 3.6). The supremum over levels is essential: one near-optimal level
can miss many separated wells. With quadratic growth away from the optimal set and the specified

                                                      5
density conditions, positive-dimensional optimal manifolds give the familiar ϵ−p/2 rate for dimension
p. An isolated degenerate minimizer can also give a polynomial rate, so the dimension of the optimal
set alone does not determine complexity.
    Integral lower bounds sharpen this geometry on strata with controlled coordinate-projection
multiplicity. Relaxed constraints can supply further witnesses: slightly infeasible points with objective
below f∗ − ϵ must be excluded too. Under an inner-tube hypothesis, a global KKT minimizer
with a positive-dimensional active stratum and a nonzero multiplier for a relaxed constraint forces
Ω(log(1/ϵ)) all-owner events. This result excludes reductions using original feasibility. It allows
nonlinear exactly kept constraints, with a C 2 objective and compatible original exact and relaxed
constraint data, and LICQ and multipliers for that same description. Changing only the description
of the feasible set does not preserve these hypotheses (theorem 3.12).

All root-box faces can matter. For unconstrained f with a Lipschitz gradient, a lower bound
gap αqB , and pointwise upper error α′ qB , the three spatial minima and dyadic node count are
within constant factors of
                                         Z
                                               (m(x) + ϵ)− dim G/2 dHdim G (x),
                                   X
                           1+                                                                         (3)
                                G: dim G≥1 G

where G ranges over the faces of D, including D itself (theorem 4.1 and remark 4.2). This
characterization needs no quadratic-growth assumption. For analytic objectives, classical analytic
sublevel asymptotics convert each face integral into a polynomial or logarithmic rate. On a nonzero
face restriction with zeros, small sublevel volumes have order tλG logθG −1 (1/t); λG is the real log
canonical threshold and θG its multiplicity. That face contributes ϵλG −dim G/2 logθG −1 (1/ϵ) when
λG < dim G/2, and logθG (1/ϵ) at equality; positive-minimum and identically-zero faces are treated
separately. When all minimizers are interior, the full-dimensional integral suffices, and the rate is
logarithmic exactly when all minimizers are nondegenerate. At the boundary a proper face can give
a larger exponent than the full box (corollary 4.4 and example 4.9).
    McCormick relaxations have a different exactness geometry. For separately relaxed bilinear
terms, the zero-gap faces correspond to vertex covers of the interaction graph. Section 3 derives
matching, transversality, and fractional-cover bounds from this structure. A two-dimensional convex
example with an aligned optimal segment has a two-leaf exact certificate (proposition 3.21), while
an aligned three-dimensional example still requires Θ(ϵ−1/2 ) boxes (example 3.22). Alignment alone
therefore does not supply a general characterization. These results explain why convergence order
alone can miss the placement of exact regions that determines certificate size.

Changing the certificate representation. Section 5 retains a fixed factorization and local
relaxation, but allows local bag certificates connected by affine separator minorants. For a tree
decomposition with M bags and width w, global quadratic growth, C 1,1 bag objectives, vertex-
vanishing factor error, and fixed width, incidence, and conditioning parameters give a certificate
of size O(M log(M/ϵ)), with explicit dependence on those parameters (theorem 5.3). This is an
existence result using the minimizer and separator slopes. A nonconvex quartic path family has
a unique nondegenerate minimizer and an exponential spatial-cover lower bound in dimension,
yet admits an O(n log(n/ϵ)) decomposition certificate under the same separate unary and bilinear
oracle (theorem 5.4). The comparison concerns proof size. Construction, local checking queries, and
solution time carry separate costs.

Propagation and branching. Section 6 models objective-only cutoff propagation by the greatest
hull-consistent fixed point of a specified finite expression graph. Local exactness gives an ideal

                                                      6
certificate count independent of tolerance; one-sided graphs supply a sufficient condition. Conversely,
specified coordinatewise derivative cancellation with a uniform no-dominance margin transfers
geometric lower bounds through charged propagation phases. These are sufficient conditions in
different classes. The round count remains schedule dependent, and a bounded ideal node count
can coexist with a diverging number of rounds (theorems 6.5 and 6.7).
    For the exact oracle L(B) = minB (f − αqB ) on an interval, splitting at any relaxation minimizer
is 4-competitive against the smallest finished tree, with an optimal incumbent and continuous f
(theorem 7.1). Convexity is unnecessary for this counting theorem. Smooth nonanalytic examples
give information lower bounds for node-local rules, and fixed interior safeguards impose an unbounded
worst-case loss on kink families. A recentring safeguard attains the shortest safe chain that hits the
kink; its finite-tolerance bound is stated separately. In several dimensions, splitting every available
coordinate can lose a logarithmic factor even on a separable quadratic. Repeated coordinate
ambiguity forces an exponential dependence on dimension for node-local rules with coordinatewise
minimizer selection, nonanalytic germ information, and no objective-dependent information from
siblings (proposition 7.6). The rule selecting the largest coordinate contribution to qB at the
relaxation minimizer has a constant ratio for one arbitrary separable coordinate and a fixed number
of sharp coordinates, with an exact coordinatewise oracle satisfying the sharpness assumptions
(theorem 7.7). Its general fixed-dimensional competitiveness remains open.

Integer certificates. For a fixed convex projected relaxation ϕ, a required integer set P , and
threshold τ , call a class admissible if ϕ ≥ τ on its convex hull. The minimum number of such
classes covering P is the class number κτ (P ). It bounds the leaves of every P -covering convex-piece
certificate. An unrestricted semantic binary certificate attains κτ (P ) leaves when its root may
be any convex set containing P and its convex children cover the required integer points. This
existence statement asserts no efficient construction or checking algorithm (theorem 8.3). Convex
cuts preserving every required point in P preserve the lower bound. Certified incumbent removals
require an augmented leaf-and-removal count.
    The class number can be much smaller than the best variable-branching tree. For a Haar random
unimodular lattice with a uniform torus target, vanishing tolerance relative to the Gaussian-heuristic
                                                          1
squared radius gives class-number bounds between 2( 2 log2 (3/2)−o(1))n and 2(1/2+o(1))n with high
probability. They are invariant under basis changes (theorem 8.9). A separate block-curvature
family has exponentially many classes for a perspective relaxation and an exact root bound from
small block hulls.

Gaussian statistical models. Condition C1 requires every single-variable fixing that disagrees
with a specified optimal binary point to have relaxation bound at least OPT. When the relaxation
is exact at fully fixed assignments, it gives a path certificate with at most 2p + 1 nodes for p binary
variables if the optimal incumbent is available. Strict bounds above OPT give the same search bound
under exact best-bound selection when a fully fixed optimal node recovers its feasible extension
(lemma 8.12). Failure of C1 alone gives no lower bound on tree size.
    For sparse ridge regression, section 9 identifies separate planted-support thresholds for root
and single-variable certification. They are governed by 2 log p and 2 log(pλ/n) for an explicit ridge-
dependent statistic, under the stated Gaussian high-dimensional scaling and fixed margins. With
k = pγ+o(1) and n = ak log p, a suitable ridge yields a unique planted optimum, an inexact root,
and a linear path certificate when 2 − γ < a < 2 (corollary 9.5). Selected local second-moment lifts
preserve these first-order thresholds under explicit sandwich and growth assumptions. In a separate

    For binary least squares, let A = ρ/N H with H an M × N standard Gaussian matrix,
independent standard Gaussian noise, and βN = M/N → β ≥ 1. For ρ → ∞, ρ = o(N ), and
the tolerances 0 ≤ ϵN ≤ ρN , the logarithm of the minimum convex-piece certificate size and
of the minimum variable-tree leaf count is Θ((N/ρ) log ρ); sufficiently large fixed ρ gives Θ(N )
instead (theorem 10.8). A finite Gaussian nonnegative least-squares coefficient bound proves the
simultaneous C1 threshold θc = 1/[4(2β − 1)] when ρ = θN with fixed θ > 0 separated from θc
(theorem 10.6). It also yields an exact coefficient mixture and a random root threshold asymptotic
to log N/(2βN − 1) for equality of box and orthant values (theorem 10.7). This root event concerns
upper-box inactivity. Planted recovery, rounding correctness, and an integral root optimum are
distinct events.

1.3   Relation to prior work
The cluster problem in global optimization concerns the accumulation of boxes near minimizers.
Earlier analyses relate this accumulation to the convergence order and constants of bounding
schemes (Du and Kearfott, 1994; Wechsung et al., 2014). Constrained analyses also identify nearly
feasible points with low objective values as a source of clustering (Kannan and Barton, 2017). Our
profile comparison and root-face characterization address the size of adaptive certificates, with
lower bounds applying to every valid cover and constructive upper bounds realized by subdivision.
The nonlinear constraint-gap argument retains the original exact/relaxed partition and makes the
operation restrictions explicit.
    Instance-dependent certified Lipschitz optimization also uses near-optimal covering numbers
(Bachoc et al., 2021). That work measures function evaluations under a Lipschitz oracle. Here the
local relaxation specifies the regions that can be certified and the relevant resolution. Classical
analytic singularity theory supplies the sublevel-volume asymptotics used in section 4 (Lin, 2017);
our result connects these asymptotics on all box faces to spatial certificate sizes.
    McCormick and multilinear envelope formulas are established tools (McCormick, 1976; Rikun,


                                                    8
1997). We use them to relate gap-zero faces and graph projections to certificate counts. Interval
overestimation and validated exclusion regions are also established tools (Schichl et al., 2014). Our
objective-cutoff propagation analysis fixes the expression graph and distinguishes its ideal bound
from its round cost. Competitive comparison with an optimal certificate has analogues in other
approximation problems, including analysis of the chord algorithm (Daskalakis et al., 2016). Our
branching results compare tree counts under an exact quadratic-gap oracle, with explicit information
and incumbent assumptions.
     Tree decompositions and reuse of conditioned subproblems are established in discrete AND/OR
branch-and-bound and weighted constraint optimization (Marinescu and Dechter, 2009; de Givry
et al., 2006). Treewidth also controls approximate LP formulations for polynomial optimization
(Bienstock and Muñoz, 2018). The focused decomposition comparison here fixes the continuous
factor relaxation and proves a separation between global-box and affine-separator certificate sizes.
Its existence and checking statements keep the costs of local optimization explicit.
     Integer branch-and-bound lower bounds already use midpoint conflicts and bounds on the
number of integer points that one leaf can contain (Dey et al., 2023). Hiding sets give a related
obstruction to small formulations (Kaibel and Weltge, 2015). The class-number formulation records
the full convex-class covering obstruction for a specified projected relaxation. It also isolates the
additional restriction imposed by variable or split branching. The statistical applications use these
distinctions to compare conflict bounds, convex-piece certificates, and path certificates.
     The Boolean and perspective formulations of sparse regression are prior work (Pilanci et al., 2015;
Xie and Deng, 2018; Dong et al., 2015). Gaussian support-recovery thresholds for other estimators,
including the Lasso, concern different optimization and certificate events (Wainwright, 2009). Our
sparse-regression results compare root certification, single-variable certification, and selected local
lifts in explicit asymptotic regimes. We also prove a narrow fixed-dimensional limitation of the
Gaussian exactness assertion in Pilanci et al. (2015, Theorem 2) under its printed per-entry noise and
ridge normalization: the true support becomes optimal, while the exactness probability approaches
a constant below one. The underlying reformulation and deterministic exactness conditions remain
valid.
     For binary least squares, maximum-likelihood recovery and box-decoder recovery have been
studied previously (Hansen et al., 2009; Hu and Lu, 2020). A theorem about rounding one relaxation
supplies no simultaneous conclusion for all wrong-fixing nodes without an appropriate probability
bound. Gaussian cone face-dimension and chi-square projection mixtures are classical (Hug and
Schneider, 2016; McCoy and Tropp, 2014). We give a direct sign-orbit proof and use a finite
coefficient tail to control all single-wrong-fixing nodes, including square systems. The resulting
C1 threshold, root inactivity threshold, and certificate-size law address distinct branch-and-bound
questions.

1.4    Scope and organization
The continuous constants can grow exponentially with dimension, and some explicit statistical
lower-bound constants make the bounds vacuous at the sizes of the archived computations. Section 11
presents those computations as finite-size evidence with their recorded solver settings, precision,
and provenance. They illustrate mechanisms and limitations; they establish no general practical
speedup.
    Section 2 defines the certificate classes and operation accounting. Section 3 proves covering,
stratified, constraint-gap, and McCormick results. Section 4 gives the root-face integral characteriza-
tion and analytic consequences, and section 5 compares spatial and decomposition certificates under
the same local oracle. Sections 6 and 7 study propagation and branching. Section 8 develops convex


                                                   9
integer certificates, followed by sparse regression and binary least squares in sections 9 and 10.
Sections 11 and 12 give the computational evidence, limitations, and open questions. Long proofs
are collected in the appendices.


2     Certificates and the work used to produce them
Fix a compact axis-aligned box D ⊂ Rn , a nonempty closed feasible set F ⊂ D, and a continuous
objective f : D → R. Write

              f∗ = min f (y),           m(y) = f (y) − f∗ ,        E(η) = {y ∈ F : m(y) ≤ η}.
                    y∈F

The function m is nonnegative on F ; it can be negative outside F . For each closed test box
B = i [li , ui ] ⊂ D, the fixed relaxation scheme supplies a set RB ⊃ F ∩ B and a projected objective
    Q

fB with fB (y) ≤ f (y) on F ∩ B. Its bound is

                                 L(B) = inf fB (z),               inf ∅ = +∞.
                                             z∈RB

For a lifted relaxation, fB (y) is the infimum over auxiliary variables at the fixed original point y.
All statements concern exact certificates for this specified scheme. They do not include numerical
errors or the cost of computing an individual bound.
Definition 2.1 (Three spatial certificate classes). A box is valid at additive tolerance ϵ > 0 when
L(B) ≥ f∗ − ϵ. Let Ncover (ϵ) be the minimum size of a finite valid-box cover of D, and let Nrect (ϵ)
be the minimum size of such a cover whose box interiors are pairwise disjoint. Let Ntree (ϵ) be the
minimum number of leaves of a finite axis-parallel split tree rooted at D, all of whose leaf boxes
are valid. These trees split a box into two closed children at one coordinate value. A missing finite
certificate gives the corresponding minimum the value +∞.
    Thus
                                            Ncover ≤ Nrect ≤ Ntree .                                       (4)
An arbitrary rectangular partition need not itself be a split tree. Refining it to a coordinate grid
may increase its size. We will obtain lower bounds for covers and upper bounds by constructing
trees, which can give the same asymptotic rate for these three different finite minima.
    A complete binary tree with ℓ leaves has 2ℓ−1 nodes. A complete b-ary tree has 1+b(ℓ−1)/(b−1)
nodes. These identities count tree nodes only. They count neither repeated relaxation evaluations
nor work performed by tightening or probing. A run with a feasible incumbent U ≥ f∗ may prune
at L(B) ≥ U − ϵ; every such pruning test is valid in definition 2.1. Upper bounds below use the
ideal incumbent U = f∗ and therefore measure certification after an optimal solution is available.
They do not measure the work needed to find that solution.

2.1   Quadratic gaps and owned tests
For y ∈ B, put

We will use aB       B 2        B          B
             i ≥ (di ) and ai ≤ (ui − li )di . If C ⊂ B, then qC ≤ qB on C. Zero-width factors
are zero. Dimension-collapsing reductions are permitted: the surviving degenerate box remains a
node, evaluated in its affine hull.

                                                        10
Assumption 2.2 (Objective lower gaps). For a fixed α > 0, the bound gap is

                                  L(B) ≤ f (y) − αqB (y)                  (y ∈ F ∩ B).                                       (6)

The stronger pointwise gap is

                                 fB (y) ≤ f (y) − αqB (y)                 (y ∈ F ∩ B).                                       (7)

Both are required uniformly over the boxes allowed by the model.

   Every valid raw test satisfies

                                    m(y) + ϵ ≥ αqC (y)                 (y ∈ F ∩ C).                                          (8)

After tightening, the same inequality may hold only on a subset of the closed test box. To handle
this distinction, an owned test is a pair (C, A), where C is a closed box and A ⊂ C is a rectangle
with possibly open endpoints. Its validity means equation (8) for y ∈ F ∩ A. Owners are Borel
and convex. An owned certificate is a finite such family whose owners cover F . It is a different
certificate class from definition 2.1.

Lemma 2.3 (Boundary-safe event accounting). Consider a finite terminating run using axis-parallel
splits and the following reductions. An original-feasibility reduction B 7→ B ′ retains F ∩ B. A
same-relaxation reduction retains

                                  {z ∈ RB : fB (z) ≤ U − ϵ},                         U ≥ f∗ .

An emptying reduction is a terminal event. Expand any stopping bound obtained as a minimum of
probe-child bounds into the finite child partition on which those bounds rely, recursively until raw
successful tests are reached. Let ℓaug count the terminal events in this augmented event tree, and let
Rrel count nonempty same-relaxation reduction rounds. Under the pointwise gap there is an owned
certificate for F with
                                         KF ≤ ℓaug + 2nRrel .                                      (9)
The bound gap alone suffices when there are no same-relaxation reductions. Original-feasibility
reductions add no owners meeting F .

Proof. Initially the root owns D. At a split in coordinate i at s, assign A ∩ {yi ≤ s} to the lower
child and A ∩ {yi > s} to the upper child. Suppose next that the current closed test is B = i [li , ui ]
                                                                                             Q

and a nonempty reduction retains B ′ = i [li′ , u′i ]. Carry A ∩ B ′ forward, including its boundary.
                                          Q

For each i, assign the discarded lower and upper owners

       A−              {lj′ ≤ yj ≤ u′j } ∩ {yi < li′ },                                   {lj′ ≤ yj ≤ u′j } ∩ {yi > u′i }.
                  \                                                                   \
        i =A∩                                                    A+
                                                                  i =A∩
                  j<i                                                                j<i

Use the closed slabs
                                    Ci− =       [lj′ , u′j ] × [li , li′ ] ×
                                            Y                                  Y
                                                                                   [lj , uj ]
                                            j<i                                j>i

and their upper analogues as tests. Empty owners are omitted. The first coordinate outside B ′
gives a disjoint partition of A \ B ′ , with at most 2n convex rectangular owners. This remains true
when B ′ has zero widths. An emptying reduction uses (B, A) as one terminal event. Induction
shows that all terminal owners partition D.


                                                            11
   A discarded feasibility owner contains no feasible point. If a feasible y is discarded by a
same-relaxation reduction, then y ∈ RB and fB (y) > U − ϵ ≥ f∗ − ϵ. For its slab C ⊂ B,
                         f (y) − αqC (y) ≥ f (y) − αqB (y) ≥ fB (y) > f∗ − ϵ.
At a successful raw terminal bound from a containing source box B, the bound gap similarly gives
f (y) − αqC (y) ≥ L(B) ≥ f∗ − ϵ. A relaxed-infeasibility test owns no feasible point. The expansion
of probing bounds ensures that each terminal successful test has such a raw source. Filtering the
partition by nonempty intersection with F proves the assertion and the count.

    Closed discarded slabs need not be valid at their retained boundary. For example, let D =
[−1, 1] × [0, 1], F = {0} × [0, 1], f = 0, RB = F ∩ B, and fB = f − αqB on RB . Exact feasibility
reduction retains {0} × [0, 1]. Both closed frame slabs contain (0, 1/2), where their q is 1/4, although
their discarded owners contain no feasible point. This example satisfies the pointwise gap. It shows
why lemma 2.3 gives an owned certificate and does not imply Ncover ≤ KF .

2.2   Which costs the accounting controls
Let Taug count processed nodes in the augmented event tree. Then equation (9) gives
                                         KF ≤ Taug + 2nRrel .                                      (10)
Consequently a geometric lower bound KF ≥ H(ϵ) bounds this combined event and reduction work.
If at most r relevant reduction rounds occur per augmented node, it also gives Taug ≥ H(ϵ)/(1 + 2nr).
Without that hypothesis it does not give a node lower bound. When no stopping test uses a probe
partition, actual search nodes provide Taug . Otherwise virtual probe tests must be included or
separately charged: a root can certify itself using many child probes while committing no split.
    There is a separate evaluation-cost conclusion. Suppose every relevant reduction round is charged
to a distinct counted relaxation evaluation, and every raw source needed by a successful terminal
bound, including probe sources, is counted among Seval evaluations. Keep each successful source
box once, and keep the discarded slab owners. This new family covers F by valid source boxes and
valid owned subsets, possibly with overlap. Its count satisfies
                              Keval ≤ Ssuccess + 2nRrel ≤ (2n + 1)Seval .                          (11)
Every surviving feasible point is covered by a successful source; every discarded feasible point has a
slab owner. A source box satisfies equation (8) everywhere on its feasible subset, so the per-test
lower bounds below apply to this new family. The count Keval is separate from KF : deduplicating
inherited evaluations need not decrease the number of terminal owners. Uncharged reductions or
uncharged probe bounds are outside equation (11). Tightening rounds, removed-piece certificates,
and relaxation evaluations remain distinct quantities.


3     Continuous geometry of certificates
The cluster problem in continuous global optimization concerns the boxes that remain near an
optimum. Its classical analyses give upper estimates for specified subdivision methods (Du and
Kearfott, 1994; Wechsung et al., 2014; Kannan and Barton, 2017). Here we first bound the
contribution of one valid test, so the resulting lower bounds apply to every permitted cover and
split tree. Near-optimal covering counts also appear in certified Lipschitz optimization (Bachoc
                                                                                          √
et al., 2021). The scale here is determined by the relaxation gap. A quadratic gap gives a ϵ + η
scale; it is not an assumption that the objective is quadratic.

                                                  12
3.1   Covering and arcsine lower bounds
Let N∞ (Y, δ) denote the minimum number of closed cubes of side δ covering Y , with N∞ (∅, δ) = 0.
A raw valid cover under equation (6), or an owned certificate satisfying equation (8), will be called
an α-valid family in this subsection. Its size is denoted by K.

its owner.

For the upper bounds, let D be a cube of side s0 . Specify the original constraint model by

      F = {z ∈ D ∩ Pex : gj (z) ≤ 0, hk (z) = 0},                v(z) = max{0, max gj (z), max |hk (z)|}.

Empty maxima are omitted. The closed set Pex contains exactly kept restrictions, and RB ⊂ B ∩ Pex .
The functions in v are the original relaxed constraint functions.
Assumption 3.4 (Second-order upper error and feasibility error bound). There are constants

            fB (z) ≥ f (z) − τ w(B)2 ,          v(z) ≤ τ w(B)2               (z ∈ RB ),                     (15)

   The distance bound is for the full feasible set, including the root and exact constraints. A
constraint qualification asserted only at global minimizers does not supply equation (16). It must
be established on the region used by relaxed witnesses or assumed directly.
   Let Tbis (ϵ) be the number of processed nodes of uniform 2n -ary dyadic subdivision, with U = f∗ .
Write sj = s0 2−j and let Nj (Y ) count closed level-j grid cubes meeting Y . A 2n -way split can

partition.

augmented event and reduction work. A statement about nodes alone needs a bound on rounds per
counted node; a statement about evaluations uses equation (11). The logarithmic comparison does
not assert that tightening takes a bounded number of rounds.

3.3   When optimal-set dimension gives the rate
Let M = E(0). An optimal set does not determine the rate by itself: flat objective growth can make
an isolated minimizer expensive. The next statement makes the needed growth condition explicit.

Corollary 3.8 (An optimal manifold). Under the hypotheses of proposition 3.7, suppose M is a
compact embedded C 1 manifold of dimension p ≥ 1, possibly with boundary, and with a finite regular

N∞ (M, s) ≥ cs−p . Apply equation (22); the geometric sum is O(ϵ−p/2 ) for p > 0.

cardinality at each level, which explains its stated contribution.

3.4    Gaps caused by relaxed constraints
An objective can be kept exactly while relaxed constraints still force large certificates. Feasible-point
objective gaps do not describe this case. Its witnesses can be infeasible points with objective below
f∗ , the mechanism already identified in constrained cluster analysis (Kannan and Barton, 2017).
For a lower bound we need such points to be admitted by the specified relaxation.
Assumption 3.10 (Inner tube). For fixed α ≥ 0 and β > 0, every test box satisfies

              z ∈ B ∩ Pex ,   v(z) ≤ βqB (z)      =⇒      z ∈ RB ,   fB (z) ≤ f (z) − αqB (z).         (23)

   Exactly enforced constraints must be included in Pex , since equation (23) cannot promise
admission of their violators. The case α = 0 permits an exact objective.
Lemma 3.11 (Owned tube dichotomy). Every raw valid certificate under assumption 3.10 satisfies

                   v(z) > βqC (z)    or   f (z) − αqC (z) ≥ f∗ − ϵ       (z ∈ C ∩ Pex ).               (24)

For runs using splits, same-relaxation reductions, and raw or expanded probe pruning, the ownership
construction in lemma 2.3 gives the same dichotomy for z ∈ A ∩ Pex . All terminal owners, including
owners with no feasible point, have count

                                          Kall ≤ ℓaug + 2nRrel .                                       (25)

Original-feasibility reductions and other independent ways to remove infeasible tube witnesses are
excluded in this run statement.
Proof. If v(z) ≤ βqC (z), then for a containing source B, qC (z) ≤ qB (z) puts z in its inner tube.
Hence z ∈ RB and fB (z) ≤ f (z) − αqB (z) ≤ f (z) − αqC (z). A successful source bound gives
the second alternative. An infeasible source cannot contain such a tube point. For a discarded
same-relaxation owner, the removal predicate gives fB (z) > U − ϵ ≥ f∗ − ϵ instead. Emptying
reductions use the same argument on their parent. The owners partition all of D, and their count
is the one in lemma 2.3 before filtering by feasibility. Filtering is not allowed here because the
witnesses can be infeasible.

    For ν > 0 define W (ϵ, ν) = {z ∈ D ∩Pex : v(z) ≤ ν, f (z) < f∗ −ϵ}. Every family of K test-owner
pairs covering these points and satisfying equation (24) obeys
                                                               q
                                           −n
                                     K≥2        N∞ (W (ϵ, ν), 2 ν/β).                                  (26)

Indeed the objective palternative is impossible, so each coordinate’s distance to the nearer test
endpoint is less than ν/β.

                                                     17
Theorem 3.12 (Compatible nonlinear constraint transfer). Let z∗ be a global minimizer. On an
open neighborhood of z∗ , suppose f , a finite constraint description of the unchanged exact set Pex ,
and every original relaxed function defining v are C 2 . Combine that description, the root inequalities,
and these original relaxed functions. Assume LICQ for all equalities and active inequalities in this
combined tuple and KKT multipliers for the same tuple. Suppose an active relaxed inequality has a
positive multiplier, or a relaxed equality has a nonzero multiplier.
    There are a neighborhood U∗ and constants c, ϵ0 > 0 such that for Y ⊂ E(η) ∩ U∗ and 0 <
η + ϵ ≤ ϵ0 , every dichotomy family covering D ∩ Pex satisfies
                                                         √
                                      K ≥ 2−n N∞ (Y, c η + ϵ).                                      (27)

If the manifold on which all active constraints equal their values at z∗ has dimension d ≥ 1, then on
a fixed relatively open feasible patch S of that manifold,
                                        Z
                               K ≥ c1        (m + ϵ)−d/2 dHd ≥ c2 log(1/ϵ)                          (28)
                                         S

for sufficiently small ϵ > 0. Neither strict complementarity of all active inequalities nor a second-
order sufficient condition is needed for this lower bound.
    The complete proof in appendix A.2 constructs constraint-preserving curves by the implicit
function theorem. It proves uniform injectivity, coordinate multiplicity, and area bounds for
their tolerance-dependent shifted patches before applying the arcsine estimate. The compatibility
requirement preserves the original exact/relaxed assignment and violation function. A LICQ
description of F alone cannot replace it. For example, let Pex be the unit ball, relax 1 − |z|2 ≤ 0, and
minimize −z1 over their intersection. The feasible set is the unit sphere and its equality description
is LICQ at e1 , but the original exact and relaxed gradients are opposite. With RB = B ∩ Pex and
the exact objective, the root is already exact and satisfies the tube condition with α = 0. No point
in Pex has objective below −1. The combined LICQ hypothesis correctly excludes this instance.
    For a run, equation (28) applies to Kall , and hence to augmented event and reduction work
by equation (25). Under the distinct-evaluation charging model, retain every successful source,
including infeasibility sources, and the discarded owners. They cover D ∩ Pex and satisfy the
dichotomy, giving a fresh count Keval,all ≤ (2n + 1)Seval . Thus the same lower bound applies to
evaluations in that model. Exact propagation of original constraints can remove the infeasible
witnesses and is outside these conclusions.
Corollary 3.13 (Finite regular KKT minimizers). Assume assumption 3.4 and that the set of global
minimizers is finite. Near each minimizer, suppose f and a finite description of the full feasible set
are C 2 , the description satisfies LICQ and KKT, every active inequality has a positive multiplier,
and the Lagrangian Hessian is positive definite on the common nullspace of the active inequality and
equality gradients. Then Tbis = O(1 + log(1/ϵ)).
    If at least one active stratum has dimension at least one and the objective bound gap holds, all
three raw certificate minima and Tbis are Θ(log(1/ϵ)). The same matching rate holds under the
tube hypothesis when that minimizer satisfies the compatible original-tuple and relaxed-multiplier
hypotheses of theorem 3.12.
    If instead there is one minimizer z∗ and its active stratum has dimension zero, a finite exact
split-tree certificate exists under the stronger vertex-vanishing upper error for some α′ ≥ 0,

                     fB (z) ≥ f (z) − α′ qB (z),      v(z) ≤ α′ qB (z)   (z ∈ RB ).                 (29)

Thus Ntree (ϵ) = O(1) even at ϵ = 0.

                                                     18
    Appendix A.4 proves quadratic growth from these KKT assumptions, the local stratum logarithm,
and sharp growth at a zero-dimensional stratum. The error bound for the relaxation remains an
independent assumption. For the feasible-objective branch, a local description of the full feasible
set suffices for these geometric facts; the tube branch specifically requires compatibility with the
original exact/relaxed tuple.
    Width-squared upper convergence alone does not give the last conclusion. For F = [0, 1],
f (x) = x, and fB (x) = x − τ w(B)2 on RB = B, the minimum at zero is sharp, but every positive-
width interval containing zero has a negative bound. A finite closed interval cover of [0, 1] must
contain such an interval; singleton tests at zero cannot cover all small positive points. Hence it
has no finite exact certificate. Under a positive objective lower gap, uniform bisection still requires
Ω(log(1/ϵ)) when a minimizer coordinate stays in a fixed middle portion of its dyadic interval at
every level. A root-coordinate position with an odd rational denominator greater than one has this
property. Merely being nondyadic does not imply it.
Example 3.14 (Isotropic and directional constraint gaps). Let D = [−6/5, 13/10]2 , f (z) = z2 , and
let F be given by −z2 ≤ 0, with Pex = R2 . Keep f exact. For RB = {z ∈ B : −z2 ≤ qB (z)}, the tube
coefficient is β = 1. The strip [−6/5, 13/10] × [−2ϵ, −ϵ) lies in W (ϵ, 2ϵ) for small ϵ. Its horizontal
projection has length 5/2, so equation (26) gives Ncover ≥ cϵ−1/2 . The upper error, dist(z, F ) = v(z),
and quadratic growth to the optimal segment give the upper part of proposition 3.7 without an
objective lower gap. The segment has N∞ (M, s) = O(s−1 ), so the geometric level sum is O(ϵ−1/2 ).
Combining the upper and infeasible-cover lower bounds gives all three raw minima Θ(ϵ−1/2 ).
    For the directional relaxation RB = {z ∈ B : −z2 ≤ aB  2 (z2 )}, three horizontal slabs give an exact
certificate: [−6/5, −3/5], [−3/5, 0], and [0, 13/10] in the second coordinate. The first is infeasible
since −z2 ≥ 3/5 > 9/100 ≥ aB  2 ; in the middle slab a negative feasible z2 would require 1 ≤ z2 + 3/5,
which is impossible; the last has minimum zero. This scheme has no isotropic tube coefficient β > 0.
Finally, exact propagation of the original inequality retains z2 ≥ 0 and makes even the isotropic
relaxation exact in one node.

3.5   McCormick gaps and graph geometry
The objective-gap hypothesis equation (6) requires loss in every coordinate not fixed by a box vertex.
Bilinear envelopes have a different zero set. In this subsection, fix the decomposition
                                              X
                             f (x) = g(x) +          cij xi xj ,   F = D ∩ P,                       (30)
                                              ij∈G

where g is convex and continuous, P is a polyhedron in original variables, G has an edge ij for
every nonzero coefficient, and each bilinear term is relaxed separately by its convex envelope on B.
The polyhedron is kept exactly. Write fB = f − ΓB for this relaxation, so
                     L(B) ≥ f∗ − ϵ      ⇐⇒      ΓB (x) ≤ m(x) + ϵ (x ∈ F ∩ B).                      (31)
The fixed decomposition matters even when the full objective is convex. For an owned test (C, A)
in this subsection, validity means ΓC (x) ≤ m(x) + ϵ for x ∈ A ∩ F . This is the McCormick validity
condition, rather than the isotropic q condition used earlier. Every owned-family statement below
requires this condition. The formulas below are the classical bilinear-envelope formulas (McCormick,
1976); we give their derivation to identify the geometric properties used by the certificate bounds.
Lemma 3.15 (Bilinear gaps and exact faces). Put δi− = xi − li and δi+ = ui − xi on B. The gap
for the term cij xi xj is

Moreover ΓB = ij∈G γij,B vanishes at x precisely when the coordinates of x at box endpoints form
a vertex cover of G. Thus it vanishes on an entire coordinate face precisely when that face’s free
coordinates form an independent set.

Proof. Normalize the two nondegenerate intervals to s, t ∈ [0, 1], removing affine terms. The
convex envelope of st is max(0, s + t − 1). For s + t ≤ 1, express (s, t) as a convex combination
of (0, 0), (1, 0), (0, 1), whose weighted product is zero. For s + t ≥ 1, use (1, 0), (0, 1), (1, 1), whose
weighted product is s + t − 1. Every convex underestimator is bounded above by these combinations,
and the two affine minorants are valid. Similarly the concave envelope is min(s, t), using, when
s ≤ t, weights s, t − s, 1 − t on (1, 1), (0, 1), (0, 0) and reversing coordinates otherwise. Scaling and
the sign of the coefficient give equation (32).
    Each endpoint distance is at least its nearest-endpoint distance; choosing the product involving
the nearest endpoint in one coordinate gives equation (33). q          The product of the two candidate
                                  B  B

most wi wj /4, with equality at the common interval centers. Degenerate intervals give zero directly.
Each edge gap is nonnegative and vanishes exactly when one endpoint coordinate is fixed; their sum
therefore has the claimed graph zero set.

All edge maxima occur at the same box center. The first inequality supplies a vertex-vanishing
upper error, but supplies no positive isotropic objective lower gap.
    A joint envelope of the bilinear sum is pointwise stronger than the sum of separate envelopes. It
has the same exact-face criterion: any convex representation of a point on a box face uses points of
that face, by a supporting affine functional. Its restriction is therefore the envelope of the restricted
sum. That restricted bilinear sum is convex exactly when it is affine: a surviving free edge has the
indefinite Hessian submatrix ( 0c 0c ). Sums of termwise edge losses below are not claims about a joint
envelope.
    The ownership and cost construction also applies to this model. Indeed, restricting a convex
underestimator from B to C ⊂ B gives an admissible underestimator on C, so each child envelope is
at least the restricted parent envelope. Hence ΓC ≤ ΓB on C. At a discarded same-relaxation feasible
point, fB (x) > U − ϵ gives ΓB (x) < m(x) + ϵ, and therefore the owned child test is McCormick-valid.
Successful containing source bounds give the weak inequality in the same way. Original-feasibility
owners contain no feasible point. Thus the rectangular ownership proof gives KF ≤ ℓaug + 2nRrel for
this validity condition as well. The augmented-node and separately charged evaluation conclusions
in equations (10) and (11) follow with the same distinct costs. They do not require an isotropic
objective gap.

Proposition 3.17 (Curved transversal strata). Let S ⊂ F be a compact subset of an embedded C 1
p-manifold, and suppose every tangent space is graph-transversal for the selected graph H above. If

                                     K ≥ cS Hp (S)(α/(η + ϵ))p/2 .

In particular an optimal stratum of positive measure forces Ω(ϵ−p/2 ) tests.

Proof. For each vertex cover J0 , injectivity on each tangent space gives locally a p-coordinate
projection with coordinates in J0 and an inverse chart of bounded derivative. A finite chart cover
gives
                                 Hp (S ∩ {|xJ0 − ξ|∞ ≤ r}) ≤ Crp
uniformly in ξ and small r. This follows by projecting each chart intersection into the p chosen
coordinatep intervals and bounding the inverse chart’s area factor. At an owned point of a valid test,

cover and contains a minimal cover J0 . The point’s J0 coordinates lie within ρ of one of that test’s
2|J0 | endpoint combinations. Sum the slab measure estimate over the finitely many minimal covers
and endpoint choices. Each test contributes at most C ′ ρp measure, proving the assertion.

Proof. The preimage of a test box is a rectangle r [αr , βr ], because the pairs are disjoint. On the

Its envelope is no greater than the endpoint chord, so its gap at tr is at least κr (tr − αr )(βr − tr ).

    For one such feasible chord through a minimizer, z∗ + tv with |t| ≤ t0 and t0 > 0, an upper

Proposition 3.20 (One-sided concave ray). Suppose z∗ + tv is feasible for 0 ≤ t ≤ t0 , with t0 > 0,
a selected edge satisfies cij vi vj < 0, and lim inf t↓0 m(z∗ + tv)/t = 0. Then Ncover (ϵ) → ∞ as ϵ ↓ 0,
including for constrained problems.
Proof. If covers with at most N boxes existed along ϵℓ ↓ 0, their intersections with the ray would
give at most N closed intervals covering [0, t0 ]. Pad empty intersections by singleton intervals and
pass to a subsequence along which all endpoints converge. The limit intervals still cover [0, t0 ],
since the distance of any point to their union is the limit of its distances to the finite unions. For a
limiting interval [a, b] and a < t < b, endpoint interpolation of the concave selected edge gives

                              m(z∗ + tv) + ϵℓ ≥ |cij vi vj |(t − aℓ )(bℓ − t).

For large ℓ, t lies in that interval. Passing to the limit gives the same inequality at tolerance
zero. Among finitely many limit intervals covering points arbitrarily close to zero, one has left
endpoint zero and right endpoint b > 0. Consequently m(z∗ + tv)/t ≥ |cij vi vj |(b − t) for 0 < t < b,
contradicting the liminf assumption.

Proposition 3.21 (A full aligned segment in two dimensions). Let f (x, y) = g(x, y) + cxy, with
c ̸= 0 and g convex and continuous on [Lx , Ux ] × [Ly , Uy ]. If the entire segment {a} × [Ly , Uy ] is
optimal, cutting at x = a gives an exact certificate with at most two leaves. If a is an endpoint, the
root is exact. The assertion uses the termwise model equation (30) with no other constraints.
   Appendix A.6 proves this statement using convexity to identify the transverse slope along the
segment. Its full-length hypothesis matters. Alignment alone is insufficient in higher dimensions, as
the following example shows.
Example 3.22 (An aligned three-dimensional segment). On D = F = [0, 1]3 , fix a ∈ (0, 1) and set

                              f = (x − a)2 + (x − a)(y − z) + (y − z)2 .

Use the specified decomposition g = (x − a)2 + (y − z)2 − a(y − z) and ϕ = xy − xz, with graph
G = {xy, xz}. The function g is convex. Writing X = x−a and Y = y−z gives f = X 2 +XY +Y 2 ≥
(X 2 + Y 2 )/2, so the optimum is zero and its set is the segment {(a, t, t) : 0 ≤ t ≤ 1}. Its tangent
lies in the independent coordinates {y, z} and its line constant τ is zero. Nevertheless

                                    Ncover ≍ Nrect ≍ Ntree ≍ ϵ−1/2 .

For the lower bound consider the strip {(x, t, t) : |x − a| ≤ h, 0 ≤ t ≤ 1}, with parameter area
2h. On it m ≤ h2 . A valid test meets the strip in a parameter rectangle. At its center the two
edge lower bounds give gap at least wx wt /2, so its area is at most 2(h2 + ϵ). Summing and taking
      √                                      √
h = ϵ ≤ min(a, 1 − a) gives Ncover ≥ 1/(2 ϵ).
     For the upper bound, a dyadic cube of side s has ΓB ≤ s2 /2. An unpruned cube therefore
contains a point with |x − a| < s and |y − z| < s. At level j at most four x-intervals and at most
5 · 2j pairs of y, z intervals can do so. There are O(2j ) unpruned cubes. All cubes are valid when
s2j /2 ≤ ϵ. The geometric sum of active-parent counts up to this level gives O(ϵ−1/2 ) processed nodes,
and coordinate cuts realize the dyadic construction. The fixed decomposition thus gives diverging
certificates even for this convex quadratic objective.

                                                    23
4    Box-face integrals and analytic singularities
For an unconstrained smooth objective, the root-box faces give a complete description of certification
complexity for a strictly positive separable quadratic gap. The description includes the full box.
It also includes lower-dimensional faces, because a minimizer on the boundary can be sharp in its
inward normal direction and flat along the boundary. A volume integral over the full box can then
miss the part of the domain that requires the most subdivision.

                                                                                     
                                                                                      
                      Ncover (ε) ≥ max 1, max               αi                   IF (ε) .           (45)
                                       F ∈F+ (D) π dF
                                      
                                                       i∈JF
                                                                                      
                                                                                      

There are finite constants C0 , C1 , independent of ε, such that
                                                            X
                                 |Tdyad (ε)| ≤ C0 + C1                IF (ε).                       (46)
                                                          F ∈F+ (D)

The same dyadic partition has a binary split-tree realization with at most a constant multiple of this
number of nodes.
    The proof is in appendix B.3. Its lower bound is an arcsine integral on each root face. For
the upper bound, an invalid box contains a point with m + ε of order its squared side length.
Nonnegativity of m on the closed root box controls the derivative toward a nearby face, even
when there is no room for an outward step. Moving the point to that face gives a relative cube of
comparable size inside a slightly larger sublevel set. Packing these relative cubes and summing their
scales gives the face integrals. Optimal vertices require a separate argument: either every inward
edge derivative is positive and the corner box is certified after a fixed refinement, or an edge integral
accounts for its remaining levels. No separate log(1/ε) term is needed for vertices.
    The theorem assumes neither quadratic growth nor analyticity. It also does not identify the finite
values of the three certificate minima. The comparisons follow from a lower bound for arbitrary
covers and an upper bound constructed by a tree. A total-node count and a leaf count differ by
a fixed factor for these finite trees; additional relaxation evaluations or reduction rounds remain
separate costs.
Remark 4.2 (Gap assumptions and owned certificates). The proof uses only two properties of the
oracle: a bound implying m + ε ≥ αqB on every valid box, with α > 0, and an upper-error bound
fB ≥ f − α′ qB , with α′ > 0 and qB = i (xi − ai )(bi − xi ). Thus the characterization also holds
                                         P

under these two bounds, with modified constants. Strict positivity is essential to this scope. For
example, choosing zero correction for a convex objective gives an exact root bound and can give a
one-box certificate.
   The per-face lower bound also applies to test boxes Be with measurable owned sets Ae ⊆ Be
covering D, when (41) is known only on Ae . The integral over each owned part is bounded by the
same full-box arcsine integral. The event accounting from the certificate framework can therefore
transfer this lower bound to allowed reductions. Such a transfer bounds the specified event or
evaluation cost; it does not identify an owned family with a valid closed-box cover.

4.1    Analytic objectives and exact power–logarithm rates
Suppose now that f is real analytic on a neighborhood of D. On a face F ∈ F+ (D) with a nonzero
restriction h = m|F and minF h = 0, define its real log canonical threshold and multiplicity (λF , θF )
through the first positive pole and its order in
                                                 Z
                                      ζF (z) =       h(x)−z dσF (x).                                (47)
                                                 F

The integral initially converges for sufficiently small positive real z, and the definition uses its
meromorphic continuation. The restriction must be considered on F as a domain in its own affine
hull. A boundary zero is included, and no nonnegativity outside F is required.

                                                     25
   The analytic input is classical. On these compact semianalytic domains, λF is a positive rational
number, θF is a positive integer, and
                                  Z
                  ZF (s) :=            e−sh(x) dσF (x) ∼ cZ,F s−λF (log s)θF −1 ,         cZ,F > 0.   (48)
                                  F

This is the compact-domain asymptotic of Lin (2017, Section 2, Theorem 2.10). His domain
treatment resolves the analytic function and the boundary inequalities together. A closed face has
affine analytic boundary inequalities in its intrinsic coordinates and density one, so it satisfies those
hypotheses. His absolute-value convention agrees with (47), because h ≥ 0 on F . A restriction that
is identically zero is handled separately below; it has no ordinary positive-pole RLCT pair of this
form.
Lemma 4.3 (Evaluation of the regularized integral). Let h ≥ 0 be a nonzero analytic function on
a positive-dimensional compact box F , with a zero onR F , and suppose its Laplace integral satisfies
(48) with parameters λ, θ, cZ . For a > 0, put Ia (ε) = F (h + ε)−a dσF . Then

Proof. The gamma integral and Tonelli’s theorem give

Let p = θ − 1. By the Laplace asymptotic and boundedness of ZF near zero, for every s > 0,
ZF (s) ≤ Cs−λ (1 + log(2 + s))p . If a > λ, substitute s = u/ε in (52). For each u > 0, the integrand
divided by cZ ελ−a (log(1/ε))p converges to ua−λ−1 e−u . For ε ≤ e−1 it is bounded by a constant
times ua−λ−1 e−u (2 + log(2 + u))p . This function is integrable. Dominated convergence gives (49).
    If a = λ, the part of the integral below a fixed S > 1 is bounded. Above S, use ZF (s) =
cZ s−a (log s)p (1 + o(1)). Writing L = log(1/ε), one has

Indeed, replacing e−εs by one on [S, 1/ε] changes the integral by at most ε S (log s)p ds = O(Lp ).
On [1/ε, ∞), the substitution u = εs bounds the integral by O(Lp + 1). Bounding the o(1) remainder
above a sufficiently large fixed S by an arbitrary constant proves (50). Finally, if a < λ, the integrand
in (52) with ε = 0 is integrable both at zero and infinity. Tonelli and monotone convergence give
(51).

Corollary 4.4 (Facewise analytic rate). Under the assumptions of theorem 4.1, with f real analytic
near D, each certificate quantity and the dyadic node count satisfy
                                                                       (                 )

Proof. Apply lemma 4.3 with a = dF /2 whenever the restriction is nonzero and has a zero. If
m|F ≡ 0, its integral is exactly σF (F )ε−dF /2 . If minF m > 0, its integral converges to a positive
finite value. There are finitely many faces, so their sum is comparable to the largest term, with a
baseline one. Now use theorem 4.1.

    The equality case λF = dF /2 has one more logarithm than the Laplace asymptotic. The theorem
retains this case even when θF > 1; it needs no claim about whether such a face can dominate all of
its subfaces. The constants in lemma 4.3 are leading constants for the integrals. They do not assert
that a discrete tree count has a limiting leading constant.

4.2   When the full-dimensional integral suffices
Proposition 4.5 (Full-box reduction). Under the assumptions of theorem 4.1, either of the following
conditions implies

                     Ncover (ε) ≍ Nrect (ε) ≍ Ntree (ε) ≍ |Tdyad (ε)| ≍ 1 + ID (ε) :             (55)

  (i) Every minimizer lies in the interior of D.
 (ii) m is nonnegative on a fixed open neighborhood of D, and its gradient is Lipschitz on a smaller
      closed neighborhood of D.
Proof. In case (i), compactness gives min∂D m > 0, so every proper face integral is bounded as ε ↓ 0.
Case (ii) is proved in appendix B.4: nonnegativity beyond the box makes low-sublevel points on
each face centers of full-dimensional inward cubes of side proportional to the square root of the
sublevel height. Packing these cubes and integrating their sublevel volumes gives IF ≤ C(1 + ID )
for every proper face. In either case, use theorem 4.1.

Corollary 4.6 (Interior analytic minimizers). Suppose f is analytic near D and every minimizer is
interior. Let (λ, θ) be the RLCT pair on D. Then λ ≤ n/2. If λ < n/2, all the quantities in (55)
have rate ελ−n/2 (log(1/ε))θ−1 . Equality λ = n/2 holds exactly when every minimizer has positive
definite Hessian. In that case the minimizers are finite, θ = 1, and the rate is log(1/ε). Moreover,
                                                                          
                                (2π)n/2
                                                         det(∇2 f (x))−1/2  log(1/ε).
                                              X
                     ID (ε) ∼                                                                   (56)
                                Γ(n/2)    x∈arg minD f

Proof. At an interior minimizer x∗ , the gradient vanishes and the Hessian is positive semidefinite.
Taylor’s
 √       theorem gives m(x∗ + h) ≤ C∥h∥2 , so the sublevel set of height t contains a ball of radius
c t. Hence ZD (s) ≥ cs−n/2 and λ ≤ n/2. If the Hessian has a unit null vector v, write h = w + rv
with w ⊥ v. Analyticity gives, for small h,

                                  m(x∗ + w + rv) ≤ C(∥w∥2 + |r|3 ).

For example, this follows by bounding the quadratic term on v ⊥ and the Taylor remainder O(∥h∥3 ).
The corresponding anisotropic sublevel box has volume at least ct(n−1)/2+1/3 . Thus ZD (s) ≥
cs−((n−1)/2+1/3) , forcing λ ≤ (n − 1)/2 + 1/3 < n/2.

                                                    27
    If all Hessians are positive definite, each minimizer is isolated. Their closed set is compact, and
an infinite set would have a nonisolated accumulation point, so there are finitely many. Outside
fixed disjoint neighborhoods of these points, m has a positive lower bound. At each minimizer,
rescale h = s−1/2 z in its Laplace integral. The local quadratic lower bound gives an integrable
Gaussian majorant, and Taylor expansion and dominated convergence give

                         ZD (s) ∼ (2π)n/2 s−n/2                   det(∇2 f (x))−1/2 .
                                                          X

                                                   x∈arg minD f

Therefore λ = n/2 and θ = 1. Apply lemma 4.3 and proposition 4.5 to obtain the stated rate and
(56).

    Under condition (ii) of proposition 4.5, the same full-dimensional RLCT rate applies when m is
nonzero. Every zero is then a critical point in an open neighborhood. A small ball centered at that
zero meets D in a set containing a fixed fraction of its volume, so the preceding quadratic upper
bound again gives λ ≤ n/2. Boundary degeneracy need not satisfy the interior Hessian equivalence
just proved.

4.3   Examples and the role of the boundary
Example 4.7 (An isolated degenerate minimum). On D = [−1, 1], let m(x) = x4 and fix a positive
coefficient in (40). The minimum is isolated, but
                                              Z ε−1/4
                            ID (ε) = ε−1/4              (1 + z 4 )−1/2 dz ≍ ε−1/4 .
                                               −ε−1/4

The limiting integral over R is finite and positive. Thus all three certificate complexities have rate
ε−1/4 , although the optimal set has dimension zero. Replacing x4 by x2 gives the critical logarithmic
rate. A rule based only on the dimension of the optimal set therefore requires a growth hypothesis,
such as quadratic growth; it does not apply to arbitrary isolated analytic minima.

Example 4.8 (A singular intersection gives a logarithmic factor). Let m(x, y) = x2 y 2 on [−1, 1]2 .
This function is nonnegative on R2 , so proposition 4.5 applies. Writing t = δ 2 , the sublevel area for
0 < δ < 1 is exactly                               !

regularized integral can also be evaluated directly:
                                       Z 1          √

Example 4.9 (The full-box RLCT misses the dominant face). On the four-dimensional cube

              D = [0, 0.9] × [−0.4, 0.5]3 ,       m(x, y, z, w) = x(1 − x) + y 4 + z 4 + w4 ,



                                                        28
the unique minimizer is the origin, on the face F = {x = 0} ∩ D. Taking αi = 1 in (40) makes every
node underestimator convex, since the Hessian of m is diag(−2, 12y 2 , 12z 2 , 12w2 ).
    The Laplace integral factors. For the first coordinate, substituting x = r/s and using x(1 − x) ≥
x/10 gives
                                         Z 0.9

                                          0

For each quartic coordinate, substituting y = s−1/4 v gives
                      Z 0.5                                        Z
                                  4                                       4
                              e−sy dy ∼ c4 s−1/4 ,          c4 =       e−v dv = 2Γ(5/4).
                       −0.4                                        R

Consequently the full-box pair is (λD , θD ) = (7/4, 1), and ID (ε) ≍ ε−1/4 . On F , the pair is (3/4, 1)
and dF = 3, so IF (ε) ≍ ε−3/4 . Every proper face other than F has positive minimum: it fixes
x = 0.9 or at least one quartic coordinate at a nonzero endpoint. The face characterization therefore
gives
                         Ncover (ε) ≍ Nrect (ε) ≍ Ntree (ε) ≍ |Tdyad (ε)| ≍ ε−3/4 .
This also explains why nonnegativity merely on the root box is enough for the facewise theorem but
not for the full-box reduction: x(1 − x) becomes negative immediately outside the face x = 0.

   The analytic input describes how sublevel measure concentrates near a singular zero. The
additional optimization argument identifies which intrinsic dimension must be paired with that
concentration. For this oracle it is the maximum over root-box faces, and at boundary minima that
maximum can be attained on a proper face. For face-exact relaxations such as termwise McCormick
bounds, the gap has a different zero set; the geometric tools in the preceding section apply to that
separate setting.


5     Changing the certificate representation
A spatial tree describes its final certificate by boxes in all the original variables. A decomposition
certificate can reuse local boxes across many combinations of those variables. The distinction
matters even when both certificates use exactly the same factor relaxations. We show this on a
nonconvex path problem with a unique, nondegenerate interior minimum: every spatial certificate
is exponentially large in the dimension, whereas a decomposition certificate has size O(n log(n/ϵ)).
The comparison concerns certificate size. It does not charge the work of finding the minimizer or
identify a local convex program with a spatial branch-and-bound node.
    Decomposition and the reuse of independent subproblems are established ideas in AND/OR
search and branch-and-bound (Marinescu and Dechter, 2009). Width also controls approximate
formulations for polynomial optimization (Bienstock and Muñoz, 2018). Our purpose here is to
isolate what changes when the local relaxation is fixed and the representation of its certificate
changes.

5.1   Local boxes and separator minorants

Definition 5.1 (Decomposition certificate). For each nonroot bag, choose a rectangular partition

Lemma 5.2 (Validity and reuse). Every certificate satisfies ℓt,E ≤ ϕt on E and ℓr ≤ f∗ . Moreover,

                                                   X        X

                                                                  30
Proof. Induct upward from the terminal bags. For s ∈ E, choose a minimizing z in (59) and a local
leaf containing it. The child tests and the induction hypothesis give ψu,B (zSu ) ≤ ϕu (zSu ). Since
factor relaxations are underestimators, the leaf test gives ℓt,E (s) ≤ Rt,B (z) ≤ ϕt (s). The root is
identical.
    For (62), start at the root with a leaf containing xVr . Replace each child bound by the minorant
of a cell containing xSu , using (60), and then use the leaf test in a child leaf containing xVu . Continue
down the tree. Every cell minorant cancels with the corresponding child term.

    There can be exponentially many compatible combinations of local leaves. The certificate stores
their local members and the separator inequalities that justify their reuse, rather than listing every
combination.

certificate without it are separate algorithmic questions.

                                        Ncover (ϵ)    (2e/π)n/2

    Appendix C.2 proves the assertions and both spatial lower bounds. Each valid spatial box
must absorb the summed gaps of all the separately relaxed bilinear terms. A local path certificate
absorbs those gaps in two-dimensional leaves and reuses the separator information. Even an optimal
choice of spatial cuts cannot remove the lower bound. For a binary spatial tree with no additional
reductions, N terminal boxes correspond to 2N − 1 nodes. With tightening, the appropriate bound
is on the owned certification events described in the certificate framework, including the certified
removed regions; it is not a bound on leaves alone.
    The same size distinction holds for termwise McCormick envelopes in both models. The second
expression in (69) and the upper bound (70) still apply, as shown in the appendix. Thus at each
fixed tolerance in the stated range the ratio is exponential in n. The alphaBB integral bound, and
hence the uniform ratio (71), are not claimed for McCormick envelopes.

                                                         32
5.4   Size, queries, and checking work
The number of stored local members differs from the work needed to check them. For one nonroot
leaf–cell pair, (61) is a convex minimization problem of dimension at most w + 1; a root leaf needs
one such problem. For the common-slope construction, the child tests reduce to comparisons of
intercepts. If lt = |Lt | and pt = |Pt |, the number of local convex checks is at most
                                        X
                                 lr +          lt pt ≤ M (4/θ)2w+1 (J + 1)2 .                       (72)
                                        t̸=r

Only intersecting pairs need checking. If the leaves are aligned so that each separator projection
meets at most a fixed number of cells, the number of checks is instead proportional to the number
of leaves. The shell construction need not have that alignment: requiring it may refine the leaf
partitions. Its unconditional checking bound therefore contains an additional logarithm.
    The oracle in (58) supplies a factor relaxation on a specified box. One such query for each
assigned factor and local leaf suffices: its returned function is reused on all cell intersections. If at
                                                            P
most R factors are assigned to a bag, there are at most R t lt such queries. This is O(M log(M/ϵ))
for fixed parameters. If an oracle instead supplies only the result of a local minimization on
each requested leaf–cell intersection, unaligned queries are counted by (72), giving the safe bound
O(M log2 (M/ϵ)). Neither count specifies the arithmetic or bit cost of solving the convex programs.
    The factorization and oracle restrictions are substantive. Aggregating terms, adding stronger
cuts, or choosing different functional factors can change the gap and the certificate size. The theorem
is not a lower bound for every representation of (68), nor a prediction of a general solver’s runtime.
It compares two representations of a certificate under one fixed local relaxation.


6     Objective-cutoff propagation and its representation
An objective cutoff can certify a box before its relaxation is solved. Its strength depends on the
expression graph, and its cost depends on the propagation schedule. We first separate these two
dependencies. Throughout this section, the graph contains the objective alone. Revising original
constraints jointly with the objective, imposing new auxiliary-variable bounds, and branching on
auxiliary variables define different schemes. Natural interval extensions and validated exclusion
regions are established tools (Schichl et al., 2014). The results below concern objective-cutoff
propagation and the certificates it can produce under a fixed graph.
    Let D be a finite directed acyclic graph representing a continuous function f on a compact box D.
Its nodes are variables, fixed constants, and continuous operations on the compact forward domains.
Each operation has an elementary graph constraint E. Repeated references in such a constraint
refer to the same coordinate. For C ⊆ D, let Z0 (C) be the lifted box obtained by forward interval
evaluation, and let Flo (C) be its root lower endpoint. Forward evaluation is assumed inclusion
isotone and to use the exact image of each elementary operation over its input intervals. This
permits dependency loss between operations.
    For a lifted box Z, the exact revise ρE (Z) replaces the coordinates in E by the interval hull
of their projections from E ∩ Z; it returns the empty box if this intersection is empty. Other
coordinates remain unchanged. The closed cutoff revise intersects the root interval with (−∞, c].
A box is hull-consistent if it is fixed by every elementary revise and by this cutoff revise. Hull
consistency asks that interval endpoints have support within each elementary constraint. Those
supports need not agree between constraints. An exact schedule is fair when every elementary revise
and the cutoff revise occur infinitely often. For partial revises, fairness requires both the forward
and backward revise of every operation, and the cutoff revise, infinitely often.

                                                        33
Theorem 6.1 (The objective-DAG bound). There is a greatest hull-consistent box Z ∗ (C, c) ⊆ Z0 (C).
Every sequence of steps satisfying ρE (Z) ⊆ ρ(Z) ⊆ Z preserves this box. A fair sequence of exact
revises converges to Z ∗ (C, c), and reaches an empty iterate if this limit is empty. The same
conclusions hold for fair exact forward and backward partial revises.
    For y ∈ C, the minima

                                        πD (C) = min{c : Z ∗ (C, c) ̸= ∅},                                  (73)
                                                                   ∗
                                   πD (C, y) = min{c : y ∈ Πx Z (C, c)}                                     (74)

exist and satisfy
                                   Flo (C) ≤ πD (C) ≤ πD (C, y) ≤ f (y).                                    (75)
Both bounds increase when C shrinks. The limiting propagation empties C exactly when c < πD (C)
and removes y exactly when c < πD (C, y). Any allowed finite schedule removes only points satisfying
this strict inequality.

    The fixed-point viewpoint is standard in FBBT (Belotti et al., 2012). The proof for the present
schedule and cutoff model is in appendix D.1. The strict comparisons matter: a closed cutoff remains
consistent at its threshold. Relaxation pruning, in contrast, can use L(C) ≥ f∗ − ϵ. Computing the
limiting box as a node oracle does not provide a finite stopping test for every nonempty limit.

Proof. In a consistent box, the signed term interval Tj = aj Zpj has lower endpoint at least µj ,
and upper endpoint at least hj : each base endpoint must have support in its unary constraint.
Supporting the upper endpoint of Tj in the root constraint gives c ≥ b + hj + i̸=j µi . This proves

the necessary inequality.
    Conversely give the variables the intervals U , give each signed term the interval [µj , hj ], and
give the root [b + j µj , min{c, b + j hj }]. Unary constraints support their base endpoints and
                   P                 P

their term endpoints. Direct variable terms have their exact signed intervals. For the root, any
term endpoint can be supported by putting all other terms at their minima; the resulting sum is at
most Φ(U ) ≤ c. The root endpoints are attainable in a sum of intervals. Distinct root coordinates
make these root supports legitimate. This constructs a consistent box containing U . The necessary
inequality applied to Πx Z ∗ proves both formulas. Continuity of term minima and endpoint values,
on the compact set of subbox endpoints, proves attainment.

subinterval crossing zero gives Φ ≥ − maxU x2 ≥ −1. The single-node square representation has
bound zero. Moreover the formula does not apply to the root equation w = x + x − x: its repeated
references give the exact equation w = x, not three independent root coordinates.

     Thus exposing a coordinate such as z = x1 − x2 can change certification. For example h(z) =

Theorem 6.5 (Local exactness and ideal node counts). Assume F = D, f is L-Lipschitz in the sup
norm, and L(C) ≥ minC f − τ w(C)2 , where w(C) is the largest side length. Suppose a relatively
open set R contains the minimizing set and, for some w0 > 0, πD (C) ≥ f∗ for all C ⊆ R with
w(C) ≤ w0 . An ideal algorithm with incumbent f∗ first decides whether the objective fixed point at
cutoff f∗ − ϵ is empty, then optionally contracts to a box containing its variable projection, then
applies the relaxation test and widest-side bisection. Its node count is bounded independently of
ϵ > 0.
Proof. If R = D, boxes of width at most w0 are pruned by propagation. Otherwise δ = minD\R (f −
f∗ ) > 0. Choose 0 < h ≤ w0 with Lh ≤ δ/2 and τ h2 ≤ δ/2. A box of width at most h is either
contained in R, or contains a point outside R. In the latter case f − f∗ ≥ δ/2 throughout it, so
any contracted box passes the relaxation test. Thus no such box is split. The integer potential
   i max{0, ⌈log2 (wi (C)/h)⌉} never increases under contraction and falls by at least one at each split.
P

If its root value is d, the tree has at most 2d+1 − 1 nodes.

                                                       35
    This theorem concerns the fixed-point oracle. Its decision may require many rounds. It is the
exact-bound mechanism behind absence of clustering (Du and Kearfott, 1994; Wechsung et al.,
2014), applied to a graph-dependent bound. No representation-free node lower bound can hold in
this unconstrained model: the representation f∗ + |f − f∗ | equals f on D and has forward lower
bound f∗ . It encodes the answer in its constant f∗ . For a constrained optimum this identity need
not hold at infeasible points.

    The full event and inheritance proof is in appendix D.2. In particular, shared closed boundaries

                                      X
                           ℓ(y) =         ∥∇tj (y)∥1 − 2 max ∥∇tj (y)∥1 > 0.
