                                      j

Aggregate loss confines removed points to thin face layers, rather than to vertex neighborhoods.
Under uniform aggregate loss, an interior nondegenerate minimum in dimension n ≥ 2 still forces
a logarithmic event count; a minimizing arc whose tangent has every coordinate bounded away
from zero still forces Ω(ϵ−1/2 ) events. Precise hypotheses and complete proofs are in appendix D.3.
These weaker hypotheses do not establish the general ϵ−p/2 rate in dimension p ≥ 3.
    Finally, the node reduction in theorem 6.5 can move work into propagation rounds. For the graph
s2 − 2as + a2 on a fixed interval around a > 0, a forward-cutoff-backward schedule needs Ω(aϵ−1/2 )
rounds before emptying, although its limiting bound is exact. For u = t2 , v = u2 , f = u − 2v, a
schedule with initial 0 < U0 < 1/2 on u empties in O(log log(1/ϵ)) rounds. Appendix D.4 proves
both statements for the specified schedules. Neither is a bound for all graphs or all schedules.
General criteria for bounded-round propagation remain open.


7    Selecting a tree from relaxation information
A small certificate does not imply that a branching rule will find a small tree. This section fixes
the oracle and compares the tree selected by a rule with the smallest permitted certificate. The
comparison counts bound evaluations. It does not count the work needed to compute a bound,
discover an optimal incumbent, query a function germ, or compute an optimal cut.
                    Q
    Here F = D = i [Li , Ui ], f is continuous, the incumbent is fixed at f∗ = minD f , and ϵ > 0.
For a fixed α > 0, the oracle is
                                                                           X
          fC (y) = f (y) − αqC (y),        L(C) = min fC ,      qC (y) =       (yi − li )(ui − yi ).   (83)
                                                    C
                                                                           i

Put Mϵ (y) = f (y) − f∗ + ϵ. A box is valid exactly when Mϵ (y) ≥ αqC (y) everywhere on C. At each
invalid node a binary rule makes a strictly interior coordinate cut and processes both children. Thus
T = 2N − 1 for a finished tree with N leaves. One exact bound evaluation is charged per node.
Validity is hereditary, because qC ′ ≤ qC on C ′ ⊆ C. Uniformly small boxes are valid, since Mϵ ≥ ϵ.
    The minimum rectangular-partition size is Nrect , and the minimum guillotine-tree leaf count is
Ntree . In one dimension they agree, and Topt = 2Nrect − 1. In higher dimensions a partition may
require refinement to be realized by a tree. No convexity of f is assumed below. Even convexity of
the underestimator in (83) is unnecessary for the one-dimensional theorem. If f + α∥y∥2 is convex,
the oracle is a convex minimization oracle; otherwise exact minimization may itself be expensive.
Theorem 7.1 (One-dimensional minimizer branching). On an interval, split each invalid node at
any exact minimizer of fC . For every valid partition with N ≥ 2 intervals, the rule has at most
4N − 5 splits and 8N − 9 nodes. If N = 1, the root is valid and the count is one. Consequently
           T ≤ 4Topt − 5    when the root is invalid,         T ≤ 4Topt    on every instance.

                                                    37
The rule terminates, and its bound permits arbitrary choices among tied minimizers, including
choices using previous search history.

Proof. Use the shifted relaxation ϕC = Mϵ − αqC . On an invalid interval its minimum is negative,
while endpoint values are positive. Every selected minimizer is therefore strictly interior. Split
points cannot repeat: an ancestor split is a descendant endpoint, and nonnested nodes have disjoint
interiors.
    Fix a certificate interval J = [s, s′ ] of length λ. An invalid node whose split lies inside J cannot
be contained in J. Suppose nested split nodes C1 ⊋ C2 both cross s, and their splits y1 , y2 lie
in (s, s′ ). The child containing s has upper endpoint y1 , so y2 < y1 . Write d = s − lC1 > 0,
e = uC1 − s > 0, and tk = yk − s. Optimality at y1 , invalidity at y2 , and validity of J give

                       Mϵ (y1 ) ≤ Mϵ (y2 ) + α[(t1 + d)(e − t1 ) − (t2 + d)(e − t2 )],
                       Mϵ (y2 ) < α(t2 + d)(t1 − t2 ),
                       Mϵ (y1 ) ≥ αt1 (λ − t1 ).

For the second inequality, C2 is contained in [lC1 , y1 ]. Cancellation yields t1 (λ−t1 ) < (t1 −t2 )(e−t1 ) <
t1 (e − t1 ). Thus λ < e: the larger node also crosses s′ . Reflection gives the analogous right-endpoint
statement.
     Nodes crossing a given endpoint are nested. The crossing argument allows at most one split
node crossing only the left endpoint of J, and at most one crossing only its right endpoint. At most
one crosses both, since its split separates the two endpoints. Thus an interior certificate interval
contains at most three split points. Each end interval contains at most one. The N − 1 interior
certificate breakpoints can each be used once. The total is at most (N − 1) + 2 + 3(N − 2) = 4N − 5.
The argument applies to every finite partial tree. An infinite run would have a finite partial tree
exceeding this bound. Continuity gives a minimizer at every node, and a uniform valid partition
supplies a finite comparison certificate. This proves termination and the node count.

Proposition 7.2 (Two-piece certificates and the known ratio range). If Nrect = 2, minimizer
branching uses at most five nodes, and this count is attained. Over all continuous one-dimensional
instances, its worst-case competitive ratio lies in [11/5, 4].

    Appendix E.1 proves the five-node refinement and gives a fully specified eleven-node instance
with a three-piece optimum. The strict instancewise inequality in theorem 7.1 does not imply a
supremum strictly below four. The exact worst-case ratio remains undetermined.
    We next specify what a rule knows. A local relaxation-information rule sees its current box, α,
ϵ, incumbent, exact bound, a selected relaxation minimizer, and the germ of f at that minimizer.
A germ gives equality on some open neighborhood; it does not allow arbitrary queries over the
box. The rule carries no objective-dependent information from other nodes. For the following root
obstruction this last restriction is immaterial, but it matters in repeated ambiguities.

Proposition 7.3 (An information obstruction on a nonanalytic class). There are two one-
dimensional continuous objectives, with convex underestimators in (83), having identical root data,
identical germs at the root minimizer, and identical functions near both root endpoints. Each has a
three-node optimal tree, but their optimal first cuts differ. For every deterministic rule using these
data, at least one of the two instances has ratio at least 5/3. For every randomized root-cut rule, at
least one has expected ratio at least 4/3. The same statements hold in a class of C ∞ nonanalytic
objectives.



                                                     38
    The construction and its smoothing proof are in appendix E.2. It uses nonanalytic functions
explicitly. For real-analytic objectives on a connected neighborhood, a germ determines the whole
objective by uniqueness of analytic continuation; this information obstruction does not apply to
that class. Together with theorem 7.1, the deterministic minimax ratio in the stated nonanalytic
information class belongs to [5/3, 4].
    Full function information can select an optimal certificate without information loss. In one
dimension define b(l) as the largest right endpoint for which [l, b(l)] is valid. Validity is closed in the
endpoint, so the largest endpoint exists. Starting at the left root endpoint, take this largest interval
repeatedly. If sj are endpoints of any optimal partition and xj are greedy endpoints, induction
gives xj ≥ sj : either xj−1 ≥ sj , or [xj−1 , sj ] is a valid subinterval of the jth comparison piece. Thus
the greedy partition has the optimal size. Sufficiently short intervals are uniformly valid, so the
construction terminates. In higher dimensions a full function oracle can choose the first cut of a
minimum guillotine certificate, and repeat on its children. This statement counts the selected tree,
not the cost of constructing the optimal certificate.

7.1   Safeguards and their unavoidable cost
A θ-safe cut of an interval of width w lies in [l + θw, u − θw], where 0 < θ < 1/2. Every child then
has width at most (1 − θ)w. Along an arbitrary path its width halves within ⌈log 2/[− log(1 − θ)]⌉
splits. This protects against relaxation points at or close to a bound. Exact minimizers at invalid
nodes in the preceding one-dimensional model are already interior, but can be arbitrarily close to
an endpoint.
    For fa (y) = 2α|y − a| on [0, 1], the exact minimizer on an interval straddling a is a, and the
interval is invalid exactly when α(a − l)(u − a) > ϵ. Every interval on one side of a is valid. If the
root is invalid, cutting at a gives Topt = 3.

Proposition 7.4 (Persistent clipping can lose logarithmically). For fixed 0 ≤ λ ≤ 1 and 0 ≤ θ < 1/2,
use the relative cut
                              σ(p) = clip (λp + (1 − λ)/2, θ, 1 − θ) ,
where p is the relaxation minimizer’s relative position. Unless (λ, θ) = (1, 0), some fixed kink
a ∈ (0, 1/2) has T = Ω(log(1/ϵ)) although Topt = 3.

Proof. The continuous function σ(p) − p/(1 − p) is positive at zero and negative at 1/2, so it vanishes
at a point p ∈ (0, 1/2). Put a = p and κ = p/(1 − p) ∈ (0, 1). Symmetry of σ gives a kink-containing
chain with relative positions alternating between p and 1 − p, and width κk at depth k. All off-chain
children are valid. The chain node is invalid when αp(1 − p)κ2k > ϵ. Thus, for ϵ < αp(1 − p), it
uses at least
                                           log(αp(1 − p)/ϵ)
                                                           
                                       2                      +1
                                              2 log(1/κ)
bound evaluations. The pure minimizer rule instead ends in three nodes.

Proposition 7.5 (The price of safety and a recentring rule). If a < θ, put J = ⌈log(θ/a)/ log(1/θ)⌉.
Every θ-safe rule, with any information and any randomization, has T ≥ 2J + 1 on fa whenever
ϵ < αa2 (1 − θ)/θ. Consequently no fixed safety margin gives a uniformly bounded competitive ratio
over kink locations as ϵ ↓ 0.
    For 0 < θ ≤ 1/3, the following recentring rule nevertheless has a bounded count as ϵ ↓ 0 for each
fixed kink. Cut at the minimizer p when it is safe; below the safe region cut at l + max{θw, 2(p − l)};
above it use the reflected formula. If d0 = min{a, 1 − a} and J = min{j ≥ 0 : d0 ≥ θj+1 }, it uses


                                                    39
T ≤ 2J + 3. Its zero-tolerance chain hits the kink in exactly J + 1 splits, the minimum among safe
chains that hit it.

    Appendix E.4 proves these claims. The worst-case location in the first assertion may depend on
ϵ; the persistent-clamp counterexample instead uses one fixed location. Recentring gives an explicit
tradeoff between width contraction and kink hitting. Its hitting optimality does not assert optimality
among all finite-tolerance certificates, since a positive tolerance can prune before hitting the kink.
All counts here are bound evaluations for the fixed oracle. They are not runtime comparisons
between numerical minimization methods.

7.2   Coordinate choice and a positive restricted extension
For a separable objective write f − f∗ = i mi (xi ), with minDi mi = 0. Let Ni (b) be the least

                       max Ni (ϵ) ≤ Nrect (ϵ) ≤ Ntree (ϵ) ≤            inf

Proposition 7.6 (Repeated coordinate ambiguity). For every n ≥ 2, there are n nonanalytic
separable objectives on [0, 1]n , at the common tolerance ϵ = 1/100 and α = 1, each with Topt = 3. For
every deterministic node-local relaxation-information rule, with coordinate-wise minimizer selection,
at least one of these instances satisfies
                                             &                 '
                                           2n+1 − n − 3
                                      T ≥2              + 1.
                                              n−1

For every distribution of such local rules, at least one instance has

                                             2(2n+1 − n − 2)
                                      ET ≥                   + 1.
                                                    n
The rules may also see the objective germ at every corner of the current box. They may not carry
objective-dependent information from other nodes or use objective information outside these germs
to resolve minimizer ties.

    Appendix E.3 supplies the family and the counting proof. The result forces exponential
dependence on dimension in any competitive constant for this information class. It does not show
accuracy-dependent loss at a fixed dimension, or apply to analytic germs or rules that learn from
siblings.
    For example, on f (x, z) = x2 in [0, 1]2 with α = 1, a rule that splits every coordinate with an
interior relaxation minimizer has Ω(ϵ−1/2 log(1/ϵ)) processed boxes, whereas a guillotine certificate
has O(ϵ−1/2 ) leaves. Appendix E.5 proves this separation. Selecting the correct point in each
coordinate does not justify splitting all coordinates.
    The ω rule instead selects a coordinate maximizing qJi (yi ), where yi is its selected exact relaxation
minimizer, and cuts there. General constant competitiveness of this rule, even for arbitrary separable
objectives in a fixed dimension at least two, remains open. The following extension isolates a class
in which the phase accounting can be completed.


                                                    40
Theorem 7.7 (One arbitrary coordinate and sharp coordinates). Scale to α = 1. There is one
arbitrary continuous nonnegative coordinate function with minimum zero on its original interval
A, and r sharp coordinate functions with minima zero at interior kinks ci . Fix a coordinate-wise
minimizer selection depending only on its function and interval. A sharp coordinate means that on
every interval straddling ci , the selected minimizer is ci with value −qJ (ci ), and on either side the
selected minimizer has zero gap and nonnegative relaxation value. Use the fixed optimal incumbent
and exact separable oracle above. Explicitly, f − f∗ = m0 (x) + ri=1 mi (zi ), and NA (b) is the least
                                                                  P

number of intervals partitioning A for which minJ (m0 − qJ ) ≥ −b.
    Set C0 = 4 and, for r ≥ 1, define

             cr = r[2r(r + 1) + 1],     Ar = 4(1 + cr ),    Cr = (Ar + 1) + 2Cr−1 (Ar + 2).

Then ω terminates with at most Cr NA (ϵ) leaves and

                                           Tω ≤ 2Cr Nrect (ϵ) − 1.

Coordinate-choice ties may be resolved arbitrarily. Every fixed coordinate-wise minimizer selection is
permitted if every sharp-coordinate minimizer satisfies the stated properties; otherwise fix a selection
satisfying them.
   The complete induction and phase lemma are in appendix E.6. The constants depend only on r
and are conservative. For an explicit sharp coordinate on [Li , Ui ], take mi (t) = σi |t − ci | − (t − ci )2 ,
with σi ≥ 2(Ui − Li ) and ci ∈ (Li , Ui ). The left and right slopes of mi − q[l,u] are −σi − (l + u − 2ci )
and σi − (l + u − 2ci ). They are strictly negative and positive respectively, so the kink or the nearest
endpoint is the unique minimizer and the sharpness conditions hold. The arbitrary coordinate may
be quadratic or nonconvex. The theorem does not cover two arbitrary coordinates or justify a global
sum over phases for general separable objectives.


8     Admissible classes for integer certificates
For spatial certificates, the permitted pieces constrain how the domain can be covered. Integer
certificates have a related distinction: a collection of convex pieces may cover the required integer
points without covering all fractional points of the root relaxation. This section identifies the least
number of pieces in that semantic model, then separates it from the cost of using variable or split
disjunctions. The same distinction explains why an integer-space cut can shorten a particular tree
without improving the lower bound forced by the projected relaxation.

8.1    The projected relaxation and the required integer points
Consider
                                OPT = inf{f (x, y) : (x, y) ∈ C, x ∈ Zn },                                (85)
where y denotes the continuous variables. Let F ⊆ Zn be the feasible integer projections, and let
fF (x) be the infimum of the objective over the feasible continuous variables with integer part x.
                                                   c ⊇ C and a jointly convex objective ϕ
A fixed convex relaxation consists of a convex set K                                       b that
underestimates f on C. Its projected value function is
                                    b y) : (x, y) ∈ K},
                         ϕ(x) = inf{ϕ(x,            c             r(Q) = inf ϕ(x).                        (86)
                                                                           x∈Q

We assume that ϕ : Rn → R ∪ {+∞} is proper. Infimal projection preserves convexity, although
it need not preserve lower semicontinuity. The convention is inf ∅ = +∞; in particular, ϕ = +∞

                                                      41
outside its effective domain K. The value r(Q) is the node bound when branching restricts only the
integer variables to Q.
    Fix a set P with F ⊆ P ⊆ Zn . It records which integer points the certificate must preserve.
Variable and split disjunctions on a bounded root box usually take P to be all integer points in that
box; branching justified only on feasible points may take P = F . These choices can give different
lower bounds. Fix also a threshold τ ∈ R ∪ {+∞}. An additive ε certificate uses τ = OPT −ε, for
finite OPT and ε ≥ 0; infeasibility uses τ = +∞.
Definition 8.1 (P -covering convex-piece certificate). A finite rooted tree assigns a convex set
Qv ⊆ Rn to each node. The root contains P . Each internal node v satisfies
                                                        [
                                      Qv ∩ P ⊆                     Qw .                           (87)
                                                    w child of v
                          T
Its effective set is Qv = u⪯v Qu , where the intersection is over its root path. The tree is a τ
certificate if r(Qℓ ) ≥ τ at every leaf ℓ.
    The sets need not be closed. Replacing every node set by its effective set makes the tree nested
and preserves (87): a required point in Qv belongs to at least one child and to all its ancestors.
The children must cover only P ∩ Qv . No coverage of all fractional points is required. This is the
semantic certificate class used below. Variable branches xi ≤ c or xi ≥ c + 1, integer splits π T x ≤ c
or π T x ≥ c + 1, and finite multiway integer hyperplane branches are subclasses when their integer
disjunctions cover P .
    A completed branch-and-bound run gives such a certificate if it preserves P and prunes only by
relaxation infeasibility, a valid computed bound βv ≤ r(Qv ) satisfying βv ≥ U Bv − ε, or an attained
relaxation optimum that is feasible for (85) and has the same original and relaxed objective. Indeed,
every feasible incumbent satisfies U Bv ≥ OPT. Bound pruning therefore gives r(Qv ) ≥ OPT −ε,
and exact integral pruning gives r(Qv ) ≥ OPT. This argument permits weaker computed lower
bounds and arbitrary node selection. Reductions that remove required integer points need the
separate accounting below.

8.2   Class number and exact semantic attainment
Definition 8.2 (Admissibility and class number). A set I ⊆ P is τ -admissible if

                                             r(conv I) ≥ τ.                                       (88)

The class number κτ (P ) is the least number of admissible sets covering P , or +∞ if no finite cover
exists. Set κτ (∅) = 0.
    Admissibility is hereditary. A cover may therefore be turned into a partition by assigning every
point to its first covering class. Pairwise conflicts provide useful lower bounds, but they can miss
obstructions that involve more than two points. Define the midpoint graph on P by a ∼ b when
a ̸= b and ϕ((a + b)/2) < τ . Define the segment graph by a ∼ b when inf [a,b] ϕ < τ . An admissible
class is an independent set in both graphs. Thus

                              ω(Gmid      seg      seg
                                 τ ) ≤ ω(Gτ ) ≤ χ(Gτ ) ≤ κτ (P ).                                 (89)

For every nonempty finite S ⊆ P with positive denominator, there is also the counting bound
                                                       |S|
                              κτ (P ) ≥                                  .                        (90)
                                          max{|I ∩ S| : I is admissible}

                                                   42
These follow by restricting an admissible partition to pairs and to S, respectively. If no admissible
class contains a point of S, no admissible cover exists and κτ (P ) = +∞. Midpoint conflicts and
bounded occupancy of a leaf extend established branch-and-bound lower-bound mechanisms (Dey
et al., 2023). Hiding sets give a related obstruction to small formulations (Kaibel and Weltge, 2015).
Theorem 8.3 (Class number and semantic trees). Every P -covering convex-piece τ certificate has
at least κτ (P ) leaves. If P ̸= ∅ and k = κτ (P ) < ∞, the semantic model of definition 8.1 attains
this lower bound with k leaves. It also attains it with a full binary tree having 2k − 1 nodes. If P is
finite, all node sets in an attaining tree can be polytopes.
Proof of the lower bound. Route each p ∈ P from the root to a child containing it and continue
until a leaf is reached. Let Iℓ consist of the points routed to leaf ℓ. Then Iℓ ⊆ Qℓ , and convexity
gives conv Iℓ ⊆ Qℓ . Consequently r(conv Iℓ ) ≥ r(Qℓ ) ≥ τ . The nonempty Iℓ form an admissible
partition of P .

     The attainment proof in appendix F.1 uses separation from the convex strict sublevel set
Eτ = {ϕ < τ }. Each class can be enlarged to a hemispace: a convex set whose complement is
convex and which misses Eτ . Successively removing these hemispaces gives a binary chain. At its
last internal node, the two remaining children cover the remaining points of P ; they need not cover
its fractional points. The appendix proves the required hemispace separation for arbitrary disjoint
convex sets, including nonclosed sets.
     The root is part of the semantic certificate: it may be any convex set containing P . This
convention matters when k = 1, since r(conv P ) ≥ τ can hold while a prescribed larger continuous
root has bound below τ . For k ≥ 2 the binary construction can begin at any prescribed convex
root containing P . For k = 1, its one-node form requires the permitted integer-preserving root
restriction. The lower bound holds without this extra permission.
     Exact attainment is an existence statement about this specified certificate class. A solver may
require disjunctions whose coverage of all node integer points has a short, explicit proof, and may
restrict itself to variable or split branching. Finding a minimum admissible partition, separating its
classes, and checking its integer coverage can be costly. Finite P makes the node sets polyhedral,
but does not bound the number of vertices or the time required to construct and verify them. This
separates certificate size from construction and checking costs, as in the continuous distinction
between covers, rectangular partitions, and permitted trees.

8.3    Cuts and certified integer removals
Theorem 8.4 (Preserved classes and removal accounting). Suppose all node bounds use the fixed
projected value function ϕ.
 (a) Convex cuts in the integer variables that contain all required integer points at the node preserve
      the leaf lower bound L ≥ κτ (P ). In particular, cuts valid for all feasible integer points preserve
      L ≥ κτ (F ), even if the cuts give the node’s integer hull. Convex feasibility reductions preserving
      those points have the same property.
  (b) Suppose a node’s effective set undergoes a finite nested sequence Q(0) ⊇ · · · ⊇ Q(s) , and every
      removal has a convex test set Di satisfying

                              Q(i−1) ∩ P ⊆ Q(i) ∪ Di ,        r(Q(i−1) ∩ Di ) ≥ τ.                   (91)

      If the remaining tree is pruned at threshold τ , then
                                                                   X
                                       κτ (P ) ≤ L + S,       S=        sv .                         (92)
                                                                    v


                                                   43
     The same bound holds for simultaneous certified pieces covering the removed integer points,
     each tested on the unreduced effective set.
 (c) For box-bound changes, one removal is one change of one integer bound by any amount. One
     pass changing each bound at most once per node gives N ≥ κτ (P )/(2n + 1). One such pass
     only at the root gives L ≥ κτ (P ) − 2n. For binary variables, iterated rounded bound changes
     give N ≥ κτ (P )/(n + 1).

   The proof in appendix F.2 assigns the removed integer points to additional certified leaves. For
general integer variables, iterated passes require the actual number S of bound changes; the proof
does not give a factor depending only on dimension. Changes of the same bound at different times
need not combine into a single admissible class.
   Integer rounding gives a clean removal certificate. For example, suppose the current set is Q
and incumbent-based tightening finds

                       ℓ′i = ⌈inf{xi : x ∈ Q, ϕ(x) ≤ c}⌉ ,      c = U B − ε ≥ τ,

with a finite infimum. Every point of Q ∩ {xi ≤ ℓ′i − 1} has ϕ(x) > c, so its infimum is at least c.
Together with the retained set Q ∩ {xi ≥ ℓ′i }, this closed slab covers the integer points of Q. Its
endpoint is one integer unit below the retained bound. Thus the retained-boundary obstruction for
continuous closed discarded slabs does not arise. Upper-bound rounding is analogous. Reduced-cost
certificates and incumbent-based probing fit (91) when their tested pieces have the stated bound on
the current node set.
    The leaf bound can fail without the removal count. Take the root [0, 1], P = {0, 1}, and
ϕ(x) = (x − 0.4)2 on that box. The integer optimum is 0.16. At ε = 0.01, integers 0 and 1 conflict,
so the class number is two. Tightening with the closed incumbent cutoff ϕ ≤ U B = 0.16 gives
x ≤ 0.8; integer rounding retains only {0}. This permits a one-node certificate, and the removed
point {1} is the second certificate piece. Strengthening the relaxation in continuous or epigraph
variables changes ϕ and can change its class number. Symmetry or dominance reductions without a
certificate of the form (91) are outside this accounting.

8.4   The cost of permitted disjunctions
Two elementary comparisons show why κτ cannot by itself predict the minimum tree size for a
restricted branch class.

Proposition 8.5 (Linear descriptions and projected descriptions). For a pure integer linear
program with natural relaxation K = {x : Ax ≤ b}, integral A, b with m rows, and objective
cT x, κτ (Zn ) ≤ m + 1 for every finite τ ≤ OPT. If it is infeasible, κ+∞ (Zn ) ≤ m. For a
rational mixed-integer linear relaxation, if τ < OPT and the projected closed sublevel polyhedron
{x : ∃y, (x, y) ∈ K, b y) ≤ τ } has a rational description with q rows, then κτ (Zn ) ≤ max(q, 1).
                  c ϕ(x,

Proof. In the pure case, use the integer points in {cT x ≥ OPT} and in {aTj x ≥ bj + 1}, 1 ≤ j ≤ m.
They cover Zn : an integer point with objective below OPT violates some row by at least one. Their
hulls respectively have objective at least OPT or miss K. In the infeasible case, only the violated-row
classes are needed. In the mixed case, scale the q projected rows to integral left-hand sides gjT x ≤ hj .
The integer-free sublevel polyhedron is avoided by the covering halfspaces gjT x ≥ ⌊hj ⌋ + 1. On each
halfspace the projected value is at least τ , since every feasible continuous extension has value strictly
above τ . If the sublevel polyhedron is empty, one class suffices.



                                                   44
   Projection can hide exponentially many classes in a short formulation. The mixed-integer linear
problem
                               n
                               X
                         min         si ,   si ≥ xi − 12 ,     si ≥ 21 − xi ,   x ∈ Zn
                               i=1

has 2n rows and projected function ϕ(x) = ∥x − 12 1∥1 . Its optimum is n/2. For 0 ≤ ε < 1/2, all
2n binary vectors pairwise conflict: vectors at Hamming distance d have midpoint value (n − d)/2.
Conversely, the 2n halfspaces σ T (x − 12 1) ≥ n/2, σ ∈ {−1, 1}n , cover Zn and are admissible. Hence
κOPT −ε (Zn ) = 2n .
Proposition 8.6 (A quadratic separation for variable branching). Let n be odd, h = (n + 1)/2,
and minimize ϕ(x) = (1T x − n/2)2 over {0, 1}n , with the natural box relaxation. For 0 ≤ ε < 1/4,
the optimum is 1/4 and the class number is two. The split 1T x ≤ h − 1 or 1T x ≥ h certifies the
optimum with three nodes. Every variable-branching certificate has at least n+1
                                                                             h  leaves. This count
is attained when each branch fixes a free binary variable and every node whose bound reaches the
threshold is immediately made a leaf.
Proof. The two sum halfspaces cover the integer points and have bound 1/4; the convex hull of all
binary points has bound zero, so one class cannot suffice. A variable node fixing a ones and b zeros
has bound zero precisely when a, b ≤ h − 1. Otherwise its bound is at least 1/4. Contract redundant
branches and make every already-certified node a leaf. At every remaining internal node a free
variable is fixed, giving the two states (a + 1, b) and (a, b + 1). The resulting leaf count satisfies
T (a, b) = T (a + 1, b) + T (a, b +1) for a, b < h, with boundary value one at max(a, b) = h. Pascal’s
                                                         n+1
identity gives T (a, b) = 2h−a−b
                              h−a   , hence  T  (0, 0) =  h   .

   This is the parity obstruction in a convex quadratic optimization form. It separates variable
branching from both semantic branching and integer splits. It does not establish a comparable
separation between general split trees and semantic trees for one convex quadratic.
Theorem 8.7 (Lattice-free interpretation). Let ϕ : Rn → R be convex and τ ≤ inf z∈Zn ϕ(z). If
Eτ = {ϕ < τ } is empty, then κτ (Zn ) = 1. Otherwise it equals the least number of closed halfspaces
covering Zn and avoiding Eτ , and the least number of facets of a polyhedron M satisfying

                                        Eτ ⊆ int M,          int M ∩ Zn = ∅.                       (93)

In particular, κτ (Zn ) ≤ 2n .
    The proof is in appendix F.3. The final bound uses the standard maximal lattice-free polyhedron
theorem (Basu et al., 2010). This interpretation connects class number to relative relaxation
complexity: the object to be enclosed is now a strict objective sublevel set. It retains the distinction
between the number of separating pieces and the cost of realizing them through permitted integer
disjunctions.

8.5    Haar-random closest-vector certificates
Let L be a unimodular lattice drawn from Haar probability on SLn (R)/ SLn (Z), n ≥ 2. Conditional
on L, let t be uniform on the quotient torus Rn /L. For any lattice basis B, use the continuous
relaxation
                     ϕ(x) = ∥Bx − t∥2 ,    P = Zn ,      OPT = dist(t, L)2 .                 (94)
Let gn be the radius of a Euclidean ball of volume one, so gn2 ∼ n/(2πe), and let λ1 (L) be the
shortest nonzero lattice-vector length. A unimodular change of basis maps integer points and

convex certificate pieces bijectively. All class-number bounds below are therefore independent of the
chosen basis. This model is distinct from a matrix with independent Gaussian columns scaled to
determinant one.

the upper exponent is strictly below 1/2. Thus an exponent 1/2 cannot hold uniformly for every
fixed positive relative tolerance.

class number. Every Zn -covering convex-piece tree inherits the class lower bound, including variable
branching in any reduced basis and general integer splits. Certified incumbent reductions inherit
(92); a claim about nodes requires its stated removal budget. Enumeration that omits out-of-radius
values also requires the two certified integer tails, adding at most two leaves per internal node and
giving a node lower bound of one third of the class bound.

8.6    Curvature conflicts and stronger block relaxations
The following finite construction places all conflicting points in the feasible integer set, and retains a
unique optimum. It shows directly how changing a projected relaxation can eliminate an exponential
class obstruction.
    Use k mutually orthogonal two-dimensional feature blocks. Block j has unit features uj , vj ,
response yj , and ridge λ > 0. There are 2k binary indicators z, with 1T z ≤ k. The perspective
relaxation projects to

and operation classes, rather than a universal algorithmic lower bound.

8.7    A sufficient condition for one-path trees
The hard-class mechanism has a useful counterpart for easy certificates. Restrict the integer domain
to a root box B = [ℓ, u], with integral endpoints, and in this subsection set P = B ∩ Zn . Let (x◦ , y ◦ )
be a feasible point of objective U , and suppose ϕ(x◦ ) ≥ U − ε. Assume its incumbent value U is
available whenever a node is processed. Write
                                   r(B ∩ {xi ≤ x◦i − 1}) ≥ U − ε if ℓi < x◦i ,
                                                                                                              (C1)
                                   r(B ∩ {xi ≥ x◦i + 1}) ≥ U − ε if x◦i < ui .

                                                             47
Lemma 8.12 (One-path certificate). Under (C1), any proper variable-branching run that prunes
nodes reaching the incumbent threshold before branching processes at most 2D + 1 nodes, where
D ≤ i (ui − ℓi ). For binaries, D ≤ n. If (x◦ , y ◦ ) is optimal, ϕ(x◦ ) = OPT, and every nonempty
      P

wrong one-sided box has bound strictly greater than OPT, the same conclusion holds without an
initial incumbent under best-bound selection using the exact node values r, provided processing the
singleton x = x◦ obtains a feasible extension of value OPT.

Proof. Every node not containing x◦ lies in a wrong one-sided root box. Its bound is at least U − ε,
by monotonicity, so it is pruned. At each branched node exactly one child contains x◦ . Hence the
branched nodes form a chain. Proper branching strictly shortens one integer range, giving its length
bound and the total count 1 + 2D. If the containing path reaches a singleton, its bound is at least
U − ε, so it can be pruned. In the best-bound variant every node containing x◦ has bound at most
ϕ(x◦ ) = OPT, whereas every off-path node has strictly larger bound. Until the optimum is found,
best-bound selection follows the containing path; at its singleton endpoint the stated processing
hypothesis supplies OPT. All off-path nodes can then be pruned.

    Condition (C1) is a sufficient certificate criterion, rather than a necessary condition for small
trees. Probing each wrong one-sided box with this incumbent fixes every integer variable to x◦ ; the
result uses one run node and up to 2n probe certificates. These one-sided classes and {x◦ } also give
κOPT −ε (P ) ≤ 2n + 1: all their bounds are at least U − ε ≥ OPT −ε.
    Even within this regime, the class number can be much smaller than the variable tree. For n ≥ 2,
take ϕ(z) = i (zi − a)2 on [0, 1]n , a = 1/(2n), and ε = 1/(8n2 ). The optimum is 1/(4n) at zero,
              P

and each wrong fixing has bound (1 − a)2 ≥ OPT. The two classes {0} and {z ∈ {0, 1}n : 1T z ≥ 1}
are admissible: the latter hull’s minimum is attained at 1/n and equals OPT. The root bound
is zero, so the class number is exactly two. A node fixing only s zeros has bound s/(4n2 ) and is
prunable only when s = n. The path to zero therefore has n branches and n + 1 leaves. This gives
2n + 1 nodes despite a two-class semantic certificate.


9     Sparse regression: different thresholds for different certificates
Sparse regression gives a statistical example in which root exactness, short variable-branching
trees, and large convex-piece certificates have different thresholds. The distinction depends on the
relaxation and on the signal scaling. In particular, failure to certify the planted support does not
establish failure to certify the global optimum.

9.1   Model and perspective relaxation
In this section, n is the sample size, p the number of features, and 1 ≤ k < p the cardinality budget.

Proposition 9.1 (Exactness at a specified support). For |S| = k, put aj = x⊤

                                                               /             i∈S

Strict inequality implies that S is the unique optimal support and that every single wrong fixing has
bound strictly above OPT.

Proof. The matrix in (103) is positive definite, and ∂j gλ (1S ) = −a2j /λ. The first-order condition
for minimizing this convex differentiable function over K says that 1S maximizes j a2j zj . This is
                                                                                     P

precisely (108). For strictness, evaluate (105) at rS . A removal of i ∈ S must replace its squared
correlation by a smaller one; forcing j ∈
                                        / S must displace a larger selected squared correlation. Both
node bounds exceed Fλ (S). Every other feasible support lies in one of these nodes, which proves
uniqueness.

9.2   Single-variable certificates
At an optimal support S, condition C1 means that all single wrong fixings are certified:

                 r({i}, ∅) ≥ OPT −ε (i ∈ S),                  r(∅, {j}) ≥ OPT −ε (j ∈
                                                                                    / S).                     (109)

Strict C1 replaces the right sides by strict inequalities above OPT. The identity gλ (1T ) = Fλ (T )
supplies the surviving singleton bound required by the path lemma, lemma 8.12. Thus C1 gives
at most 2p + 1 nodes for every variable-branching rule if an optimal incumbent is available when
off-path nodes are examined. Strict C1 gives the same bound under best-bound search without an
initial incumbent. Node relaxation solves, probing work, and their running times are separate costs.
Dual sufficient tests for fixing one feature underpin the safe-screening rules of Atamtürk and Gómez
(2020). Condition C1 here uses the exact single-fixing relaxation values.

                                                        49
    The full condition is sufficient, but need not be necessary for a small certificate. If only the
removal inequalities in (109) hold, branch on the k coordinates of S, always continuing through
their value 1. Each sibling has a certified removal fixing, and the budget forces the final node to be
1S . This is a certificate with 2k + 1 nodes. Thus failure of the forced-in half of C1 alone is no lower
bound on the minimum tree size.
    A useful dual certificate modifies the residual to cap its unusually large null correlations. Fix S,
set a = X ⊤ rS , and choose a cap q > 0. Put

      V = {j ∈
             / S : |aj | > q},     H = XV⊤ MS−1 XV ,              u = H −1 (aV − q sign(aV )),              (110)

                                                                                                    j α|.   (111)
                                                                           i∈S                 j ∈S
                                                                                                 /

The empty set V is allowed and gives ∆ = 0, Γ = 0.

9.3     Two sharp planted-support thresholds
For the easy regime, assume βi∗ = ±b on S ∗ , with arbitrary fixed signs, and fix b, σ, C0 > 0. Define

                      bn                            kb2 λ2 n               λbλ
              bλ =       ,       ωλ2 = nσ 2 +                ,      τλ =       ,      Λλ = log(pλ/n).       (114)
                     n+λ                           (n + λ)2                ωλ
The ratio τλ compares a selected correlation with the standard deviation of a null correlation.
Indeed, conditional on (XS ∗ , w), the null correlations are independent N (0, ∥rS ∗ ∥2 ) variables.
Assumption 9.3 (Easy-regime scaling). As p → ∞,
                                                                           √
                     log6 p ≤ n ≤ p,           1 ≤ k ≤ C0 n/ log p,            n ≤ λ ≤ n/ log2 p.           (115)

Theorem 9.4 (Root and single-variable certification). Under assumption 9.3, fix δ ∈ (0, 1). The
following statements hold with probability tending to one.
  (a) If τλ2 ≥ (2 + δ) log p eventually, then R = OPT = Fλ (S ∗ ), S ∗ is uniquely optimal, and strict
      C1 holds. If τλ2 ≤ (2 − δ) log p eventually, then R < Fλ (S ∗ ).
  (b) If τλ2 ≥ (2 + δ)Λλ eventually, then S ∗ is uniquely optimal and every single wrong fixing has
      bound at least OPT +λb2 / log p.

                                                           50
  (c) If τλ2 ≤ (2 − δ)Λλ eventually and k ≥ 5000/δ 2 , then all but at most n/ log2 p null features j
      satisfy r(∅, {j}) < Fλ (S ∗ ) − λb2 /2.
The negative statements compare with Fλ (S ∗ ). They imply global root inexactness or C1 failure at
S ∗ when S ∗ is optimal.

    The proof in appendix G.2 controls the true coefficients uniformly, then uses the capped residual
for the positive C1 statement. The cap changes very few null correlations above the positive
threshold. Below it, many null features jointly recover the price of consuming one unit of the
cardinality budget. A single extreme null feature need not recover that price.

Corollary 9.5 (Sample sizes and a linear-tree window). Within (115), the following bounds describe
certification of S ∗ , with fixed positive slack in their constants. When the sample size is above 2k log p,
root certification is achievable by a deterministic ridge; below that sample size it is impossible for
each allowed deterministic ridge sequence. Single-variable certification is achievable when the sample
                          √
size is above 2k log(p/ n); below this value the comparison in part (c) of theorem 9.4 holds for each
allowed deterministic ridge sequence, with a fixed sufficiently large k.
    More precisely, if k = pγ+o(1) , 0 < γ < 1, and n = ak log p with fixed 2 − γ < a < 2, a suitable
deterministic ridge gives a unique planted optimum, an inexact root, and at most 2p + 1 nodes for
every variable-branching rule under the incumbent or best-bound conventions above. For a > 2, a
suitable ridge certifies the root. For a < 2 − γ, the single-variable planted comparison fails for each
allowed deterministic ridge sequence; this does not assert a large tree.

    The ridge choice and proof are given in appendix G.3. The exact bound τλ2 < n/k for σ > 0
                                                      √
explains the sample-size constants. In contrast, λ = n gives τλ ≤ b/σ, whereas both logarithmic
thresholds diverge. At fixed per-entry signal-to-noise ratio, this ridge eventually fails both planted-
support tests, subject to the hypotheses for their converses. This is an asymptotic statement. In
particular, log6 p ≤ n ≤ p excludes the moderate dimensions of the archived experiments discussed
in section 11; those observations do not estimate the constants in this theorem.

9.4    Local second-moment lifts
The same first-order thresholds survive specified stronger relaxations when the design has many
more columns than rows. The result concerns unbounded, signed coefficients. Bounds on coefficients
supply additional information and are outside this comparison. Second-moment convexification and
rank-one strengthening have been studied by Dong et al. (2015) and Atamtürk and Gómez (2019);
the comparison below specifies the local hulls and node conventions it covers.
   Let Q = X ⊤ X + λI and define

For T ⊆ [p], let

                   HT = cl conv{(ζ, b, bb⊤ ) : ζ ∈ {0, 1}T , bj = 0 whenever ζj = 0}.                 (117)

The node bound Ls (S0 , S1 ) minimizes ℓs (z) on the face in (106). In this definition, a zero fixing

convention matters for the hard lower bound below.
    The singleton lift L1 is the relaxation with global positive semidefiniteness and βj2 ≤ zj Bjj . Every
Ls dominates the perspective relaxation and is valid for the integer problem. Also Ls dominates

 r(S0 , S1 ) ≤ R(S0 , S1 )   at every node,      R ≤ Ls

Then parts (a) and (b) of theorem 9.4, including their positive margins, hold for R. Its negative
C1 statement holds with k ≥ 20000/δ 2 and at most 2n/ log2 p exceptions. Consequently the planted-
support sample-size thresholds and the linear-tree window of corollary 9.5 also hold.
    For the positive tree statement, monotonicity of R under fixings is unnecessary: an off-path node
has R at least the monotone perspective bound of a single wrong fixing. Validity gives path-node
bounds at most OPT. Column deletion at zero-fixed nodes is therefore compatible with this theorem
if (119) holds: its upper comparisons occur only where no coordinate is fixed to zero.
    The obstruction has a precise mechanism. A perspective point is the mean of a random feasible
support. Its coefficient covariance has trace
                                                    X
                                        π(z, β) =           βj2 (1/zj − 1).                         (120)
                                                    zj >0

An exact averaged fit would pay both the ridge variance λπ and the design variance. The lift can
cancel the latter using columns with zj = 0 while paying an additional ridge cost o(λπ). Local
hulls do not detect the cancellation. The construction and both converse proofs are complete in
appendices G.4.1 and G.5.

For the hard regime, change the scaling. Let k → ∞, k/n → 0, λ/n → 0, and

For distinct S, T ∈ C, both gλ ((1S + 1T )/2) and ℓ2 ((1S + 1T )/2) are strictly below OPT −ηY , where

    For binary variable branching with certified incumbent reductions, the corresponding node count
is at least |C|/(p + 1) when each removed binary piece is charged to its node. The ℓ2 assertion uses
the retained-column node convention in (118); it does not cover deletion of zero-fixed helper columns.
    Two binary points in one convex leaf bring their midpoint into the leaf. A midpoint below
the required bound therefore prevents that leaf from containing both points. This is the conflict
mechanism from section 8; it applies to variable, split, and other convex pieces under the stated fixed-
oracle convention. The reduction count charges at most one removed piece per binary coordinate
per node.
    The proof in appendix G.6 selects highly correlated features, packs their k-subsets far apart,
and bounds all pairwise union fits at once. The integer optimum receives a separate lower bound
over all supports. For pairwise hulls, a uniform design event allows the completion of every union
using its complement as helpers; this closes the additional uniformity step required by the stronger
relaxation.
    The hard theorem treats bounded total signal-to-noise ratio. With fixed b/σ and k → ∞, the
total ratio kb2 /σ 2 diverges, so it does not overlap the easy theorem’s fixed per-coefficient scaling.
For k = pγ+o(1) and n = ak log p, (121) becomes x = 2(1 − γ)/a; the admissible pure-noise range is
a > 2(1 − γ)/x0 . The proof gives a positive exponent, not a practical estimate of it or a general
complexity lower bound for algorithms using other relaxations.

9.7   A fixed-dimensional noise-normalization check
Support recovery and relaxation exactness can differ even when the dimension is fixed and the
sample size diverges. This gives a narrow check on the Gaussian exactness statement of Pilanci
et al. (2015, Section 3.1, Theorem 2, p. 72).

                                                  53
Theorem 9.9 (Fixed dimension with per-entry noise). Fix 1 ≤ k < d, S ⊆ [d] with |S| = k, and
β ∗ ∈ Rd with support S. Put bmin = mini∈S |βi∗ | > 0 and fix σ > 0. For each n, use the Gaussian
                               √
model (101) with p = d and λ = n. Then S is the unique optimal support with probability tending
to one, but
                                                   h                  id−k
                           lim P{R = OPT} = 1 − 2Φ(bmin /σ)                  < 1,                      (125)
                          n→∞

where Φ(t) = P{N (0, 1) > t}.

    The complete proof, including the global-optimum transfer, is in appendix G.8. In the printed
                                                                 √
model, the noise variance per observation is σ 2 and the ridge is n for the unnormalized loss. The
displayed sample-size requirement

                                                σ 2 + ∥β ∗ ∥2
                                       n > c0                 log d                                    (126)
                                                    b2min

holds eventually for every fixed c0 > 0. Equation (125) therefore contradicts the claimed exactness
probability 1 − 2e−c1 n for every fixed c1 > 0 under those printed hypotheses. For example, d = 2,
k = 1, β ∗ = (1, 0), and σ = 1 give the limit 1 − 2Φ(1), approximately 0.683.
    This conclusion concerns that Gaussian probability statement. The Boolean reformulation and
its deterministic exactness condition remain the ones used above. A fixed finite coefficient-to-noise
ratio, however large, cannot make the limit in (125) equal to one. Shrinking the per-entry noise
defines a different statistical model and requires its own theorem.


10     Gaussian binary least squares
Binary least squares separates finding an optimal vertex from certifying its value with a particular
relaxation. In the Gaussian model considered here, the planted vertex is the unique integer optimum
above a logarithmic signal level. The box relaxation can nevertheless require a superpolynomial
certificate. At a linear signal level, a sufficient condition for a linear variable-branching tree has
a sharp threshold. A different, logarithmic threshold determines when the root box relaxation
agrees with its nonnegative least-squares relaxation. These statements concern different events and
different certificate classes.

10.1     Model and certificate conventions
Let M = MN ≥ N , let H ∈ RM ×N have independent standard Gaussian entries, and let w ∼
N (0, IM ) be independent of H. For a deterministic x∗ ∈ {−1, 1}N and ρ > 0, set
     q
A=       ρ/N H,    y = Ax∗ +w,        f (x) = ∥y −Ax∥2 ,         OPT =         min       f (x),   W = ∥w∥2 .
                                                                             x∈{−1,1}N
                                                                                                 (127)
Thus W = f (x∗ ) and ρ is normalized per observation. Write βN = M/N ; asymptotic statements
below assume βN → β ∈ [1, ∞). All probabilities are uniform in the deterministic planted signs.
Dividing the residual by a positive noise standard deviation σ replaces ρ by ρ/σ 2 .
    The node oracle minimizes f over the node’s convex set inside [−1, 1]N . In particular, a variable-
branching node minimizes f over the box obtained by fixing some coordinates to binary values. No
identity x2i = 1 is imposed on free coordinates. Diagonal quadratic reformulations, product lifts, and
semidefinite relaxations change this oracle and are outside the certificate lower bound proved here.

Empty classes can be discarded. This is the minimum number of leaves of a certificate allowing
arbitrary convex pieces that cover the binary vertices: a leaf containing Ij also contains its convex
hull, and the convex hulls themselves give a certificate. Every variable or general split tree using this
oracle has at least κτ leaves. Adding convex cuts valid for all the binary vertices assigned to a leaf
preserves this argument, because those cuts contain their convex hull. Incumbent-based reductions
also need certificates for the removed vertices; their cost is handled by the augmented accounting of
the integer framework, rather than by treating the retained leaves alone as a cover.
    Absorb the planted signs into the columns by writing bi = x∗i ai , B = A diag(x∗ ), and ui = 1−x∗i xi .

different nodes, but does not change this one-node independence.

10.2    Recovery and root exactness are different events
We first record the recovery range used when interpreting a bound relative to the planted value as a
bound relative to OPT.
Proposition 10.1 (Planted recovery and a value comparison). For every fixed δ > 0, if βN ρN ≥
(2 + δ) log N , then x∗ is the unique integer minimizer with probability tending to one. For every
deterministic positive sequence ρN , with probability tending to one,
                         0 ≤ W − OPT ≤ 4N log 1 + e−0.14βN ρN + 4 log N.
                                                                                  

    The proof is a first-moment calculation over flipped sets, given in Appendix H.2. At ρN = θN
with fixed θ > 0, it in particular proves unique planted optimality without a box-decoder theorem.
For square systems, the recovery comparison at a logarithmic signal level is consistent with the
sufficient condition in Hassibi et al. (2014); we use the fixed margin (2 + δ) log N . Recovery is not
asserted for every sequence of order log N .
    Even when x∗ is the integer optimum, it usually does not minimize the root box relaxation. Let
R = minx∈[−1,1]N f (x).

                                P{x∗ minimizes f on [−1, 1]N } = 2−N .                                        (132)

optimality conditions and a Gaussian exponential moment.

10.3    Finite Gaussian nonnegative least squares
The next results supply the one-node laws and the tail estimates needed for simultaneous conclusions.
They also isolate the role of selecting a support from the data. The binomial face-dimension law and
the chi-square projection mixture are classical Gaussian cone facts; see Hug and Schneider (2016)
and the master Steiner formula of McCoy and Tropp (2014). We give a direct proof because its
invariant-event identity also transfers coefficient laws. The Gaussian regression Student density
itself is classical. The finite coefficient bound below is the ingredient used for the branch-and-bound
applications.

Lemma 10.3 (An invariant-event identity). Let C ∈ RM ×n , n ≤ M , have full column rank almost
surely, and let v be independent of C. Assume the joint column law of C is invariant under every
independent column sign change. For each nonempty S ⊆ [n], put

                 dS = −(CST CS )−1 CST v,      LS = span(CS ),       z S = (I − PLS )v.

For the empty support set L∅ = {0} and z ∅ = v. Assume that almost surely every coordinate of
every dS is nonzero, and cTj z S =                           / S. Let u◦ minimize ∥v + Cu∥2 over
                                 ̸ 0 for every S ⊆ [n] and j ∈
u ≥ 0, and let S = {j : u◦j > 0}. If ES is any measurable event invariant under every column sign
change, then
                                     P{S = S, ES } = 2−n P(ES ).                            (134)
In particular, S is uniform over all subsets of [n].

Proof. For fixed C, v satisfying the hypotheses, the support is S precisely when dSj > 0 for j ∈ S
and cTj z S > 0 for j ∈
                      / S. These are the necessary and sufficient first-order conditions for the strictly
convex problem. A column sign change preserves LS , z S , changes the sign of the corresponding
coordinate of dS on S, and changes the sign of the corresponding residual inner product off S.
Exactly one of the 2n sign patterns satisfies all these conditions. Multiplying this pointwise sign
count by the invariant indicator of ES and taking expectations proves (134). Conditions indexed by
an empty set are vacuous, so the argument includes the empty and full supports.

    The explicit nonzero conditions matter: requiring only that v avoid column spans would not
suffice. For independent Gaussian columns and independent isotropic Gaussian v of positive variance,
the stated conditions hold automatically. Conditional on a full-rank matrix, each coefficient and
each off-support residual inner product is a nonzero linear functional of v. There are finitely many
such functionals.

                                                        57
10.4    A sharp sufficient criterion for a linear variable tree
Let ri be the box bound at the node fixing xi = −x∗i and leaving all other coordinates free. Criterion
C1 at x∗ requires all these bounds to reach the target. Its usefulness is monotonicity: every node
off the path to x∗ is contained in one such single-wrong-fixing node. The path lemma, Lemma 8.12,
then gives at most 2N + 1 nodes. For weak bounds ri ≥ OPT, the optimal incumbent must be
available when off-path nodes are checked. For strict bounds ri > OPT, best-bound search gives the
same conclusion without an initial optimal incumbent. The remaining singleton has exact bound
f (x∗ ), as required by the path lemma.

Theorem 10.6 (Simultaneous C1 threshold for square and tall systems). Assume ρN = θN with
fixed θ > 0 and βN → β ≥ 1. With probability tending to one, the orthant minimizer at every
single-wrong-fixing node belongs to [0, 2]N −1 . Moreover,
                                   ri − W                1
                                                               
                                                                    P
                              max         − 2θ(2β − 1) −            −
                                                                    → 0.                        (140)
                             1≤i≤N    N                  2

Put θc = [4(2β − 1)]−1 . For each fixed ϵ ∈ (0, 1), the planted vertex is uniquely optimal with
probability tending to one, and the following conclusions hold:
 (a) If θ ≥ (1 + ϵ)θc , then all ri ≥ OPT +ϵN/4 with probability tending to one. Every variable-
      branching search satisfying the incumbent or best-bound qualification above has at most 2N + 1
      nodes.
  (b) If θ ≤ (1 − ϵ)θc , then all ri ≤ OPT −ϵN/4 with probability tending to one. Thus C1 fails at
      every single-wrong-fixing node.

Proof. Here the signed columns are independent N (0, θIM ). At node i, vi = w + 2bi ∼ N (0, (1 +
4θ)IM ) is independent of B−i . Apply Theorem 10.5 with n = N − 1, a2 = θ, c2 = 1 + 4θ, and t = 2.

                                       1 + 8θ            2
a union bound over the N nodes bounds the probability of any upper-box violation by

                                      N (N − 1) M −N +2 N −2

This tends to zero exponentially, including when M = N . No independence of node events is used.
    Define the orthant value first: rei = minu≥0 ∥vi + B−i u∥2 . By Theorem 10.4, it has the marginal
law
                     rei /(1 + 4θ) | Ki = s ∼ χ2M −s ,   Ki ∼ Bin(N − 1, 1/2).
Binomial and chi-square exponential
                                √      bounds, followed by a union bound over i, give the following
estimate. Specifically, take s = 2N log N in the binomial bounds and x = 4 log N in the conditional
chi-square bounds; their total failure probability is O(N −3 ).

for every i. Hence, uniformly over i,
                                             1   1 + 4θ

which proves (140). The elementary Gaussian concentration estimates used here are proved in
Appendix H.1.
    Proposition 10.1 gives OPT = W and unique planted optimality with probability tending to
one. The limiting gap equals 12 (θ/θc − 1), so either fixed margin leaves the claimed ϵN/4 gap for all
sufficiently large N with probability tending to one. In the upper case, off-path bounds are strictly
above OPT. Before the optimum is found, best-bound search always has a node on its path with
bound at most OPT; it cannot expand an off-path node first. Once the optimum is found, those
nodes are pruned. The incumbent version follows directly from Lemma 8.12.

   This is a threshold for a sufficient criterion for a short tree. Failure of C1 does not imply a
superlinear tree lower bound. The theorem gives no conclusion at θ = θc or in a shrinking critical
window. The simultaneous assertion follows from a finite coefficient tail; a per-decoder limiting
theorem such as Hu and Lu (2020) would not by itself supply a rate that can be union-bounded
over the N wrong-fixing nodes.

10.5    The logarithmic threshold for root box inactivity
Let u◦N (ρ) be the unique minimizer of

where the planted signs have again been absorbed into HN . Let RN   ◦ (ρ) denote this orthant value,

and RN (ρ) the value with u ∈ [0, 2]N . We call equality of these values root box inactivity; at the
equality endpoint some coordinate can equal its upper bound.

Theorem 10.7 (An exact random cutoff and its first-order scale). For every realization of full-rank
HN and every ρ > 0, define

Consequently, for every fixed ϵ ∈ (0, 1) and every deterministic positive sequence ρN , root box

eventually ρN ≤ (1 − ϵ) log N/(2βN − 1), then RN (ρN ) > RN ◦ (ρ ) with probability tending to one.

Proof. The substitution z = ρ u and uniqueness give u◦N (ρ) = ρ−1/2 u◦N (1). The orthant minimizer
lies in the box exactly when its maximum is at most 2. Since the box is a subset of the orthant and

The two fixed-margin conclusions follow from the pathwise cutoff.

   A useful finite sufficient bound follows directly from Theorem 10.5:
                                                            N −1
                                   N                  1+q
                                                  
             P{max u◦N,j (ρ) > 2} ≤ q M −N +1                       ,   q = (1 + 4ρ/N )−1/2 .   (146)
                j                  2                   2

For ρ = O(log N ), the logarithm of this bound is log(N/2) − (2βN − 1)ρ + o(1). The exact ratio βN
is needed for this o(1) remainder; substituting its limit alone gives an o(log N ) remainder under
mere convergence. The finite bound and the exact mixture both apply to square systems. They
describe equality with the orthant value. They do not establish planted recovery, successful rounding,
a vertex root minimizer, or root equality with the integer optimum. The isolated value ρ = 0 is
excluded: there the objective is constant in u and the strict-convexity cutoff argument does not
apply.

10.6    Certificate complexity below a linear signal level
The lower bound is not obtained from C1 failure. It uses large families of flipped vertices and the
value of their barycenter. On coordinates where the noise gives a descent direction, spreading a
fixed number of flips among many coordinates retains the linear improvement while reducing the
quadratic penalty. A certifiable class must therefore have substantial overlap among its flipped
supports. An entropy estimate bounds the number of vertices that such a class can contain.

Theorem 10.8 (Matching safe certificate regimes). Assume βN → β ≥ 1, and let 0 ≤ εN ≤ ρN .
There are constants cβ > 0 and ρ0 (β) > 0 with the following properties.
 (a) If ρN → ∞ and ρN = o(N ), then with probability tending to one,

                                                                N
                                                                        
                                     log κOPT −εN = Θβ             log ρN .                     (147)
                                                                ρN
      The minimum number of leaves of a variable-branching certificate has the same logarithmic
      order. A static variable order, fixed independently of the data, attains the upper order with an
      optimal incumbent.
  (b) For each fixed ρ ≥ ρ0 (β), with probability tending to one, both logarithms are Θβ,ρ (N ).
In part (a), a static-order search given any incumbent value between OPT and W has the upper
node bound for certifying that incumbent, and an optimal incumbent makes it a certificate for the
stated target.

Proof outline. For the upper bound, take K = ⌈(1 + o(1))N/[4(2βN − 1)ρN ]⌉, with the o(1) chosen
positive and large enough to dominate the concentration errors. Along a fixed variable order,
simultaneously every node with K wrong fixings has bound at least W , with probability tending to
one. The number of generated nodes is then at most
                                        K
                                                  !
                                        X N
                                  1+2                 ≤ 1 + 2(eN/K)K .                          (148)
                                        i=1
                                              i



                                                      60
In the present sublinear regime K → ∞, so its logarithm is at most
                                                      N
                                    (1 + o(1))               log ρN .                            (149)
                                                 4(2β − 1)ρN
An optimal incumbent can only prune earlier. The full simultaneous proof is in Appendix H.6; it
does not require planted recovery.
   For the lower bound, Appendix H.8 proves
                                                    N
                               log κOPT −εN ≥ cβ       log ρN − log(8N )                         (150)
                                                    ρN
in the divergent sublinear regime and for sufficiently large fixed ρ. It uses (131) when x∗ has √   not
been proved optimal. If ρN → √    ∞ and ρN = o(N ), then log N = o((N/ρN ) log ρN ): for ρN ≤ N
this is immediate, and for ρN > N the ratio is at most 2ρN /N . Thus (150) gives the lower order in
part (a). The lower bound applies to all convex-piece certificates, so also to variable trees. At fixed
sufficiently large ρ, it gives Ω(N ), while the full binary tree gives the upper O(N ) logarithm.

     For example, ρN = c log N with any fixed c > 0 gives certificates of size

                                     exp(Θ(N log log N/ log N ));

planted recovery is justified by Proposition 10.1 only when its constant condition is met. At
ρN = N a , 0 < a < 1, the size is exp(Θ(N 1−a log N )). A logarithmic root inactivity threshold
therefore coexists with a superpolynomial box certificate.
    The constants in the lower proof are conservative. In particular, the subtractive log(8N ) in
(150) prevents extending the matching statement to an arbitrary interval ρ ≤ c′ N without checking
constants. At a fixed linear signal level the C1 theorem is the positive result proved here; below
its threshold, other short trees are not excluded. All these costs count certificates or nodes for the
specified oracle, and exclude the time needed to solve node relaxations.


11      Computational evidence
The recorded computations examine three consequences of the theory: the dependence of spatial
search on the tolerance and local geometry, the effect of branching points within a fixed solver,
and the separation between root exactness and small variable-branching certificates in random
designs. They provide finite-sample comparisons of specified implementations. The proofs in the
preceding sections establish the asymptotic statements; the computations do not establish them by
extrapolation.

11.1     Protocols and interpretation
The spatial studies use SCIP 10.0.2 through PySCIPOpt 6.2.1, with SoPlex 8.0.2 and single-threaded
solves. The recorded environment is Python 3.13.11 on Linux WSL2; the branching benchmarks
identify an Intel Xeon w5-2565X and Ipopt 3.14.19. The synthetic tolerance study does not record
the Ipopt version. Several solves ran concurrently, so timing reflects the shared workload. We report
node counts rather than time speedups. SCIP processed nodes, final certificate leaves, and nodes
of the separate Python algorithms are different measurements. Table 2 gives the principal SCIP
cohorts and termination counts.
    An optimal status denotes exhaustion of the solver’s search under its numerical tests. A
gaplimit status denotes its global gap stopping test. Neither status is an exact-arithmetic certificate

                                                    61
Table 2: Principal SCIP cohorts and termination counts. Each count is a run, not a distinct instance.
optimal and gaplimit are solver statuses; limit counts are unfinished observations. The 120-second
benchmark block contains only the core settings.
  Cohort                               Protocol                                      Records and statuses
  Synthetic tolerance study            20 instances; seed 0; up to 13 tol-                      2,134 total

                                                                                           1,655 gap limit
                                                                                420 optimal; 59 node limit
  Branching benchmark, first cam- 57 instances; 5 settings; 3 seeds;                              855 total
  paign                           120 CPU s
                                                                                               797 optimal

  Branching benchmark,        second   57 instances; 12 settings; 3 seeds;                      2,052 total
  campaign                             60 CPU s

  Synthetic kink study                 16 instances; 12 settings; 5 seeds;                      3,840 total
                                       4 tolerances; 60 s
                                                                             2,024 gap limit; 1,816 optimal


of the mathematical optimum. In the tolerance study, limits/absgap is set to ϵ and the relative-gap
limit to zero; ϵ is not a slack added to every node-pruning test. Feasibility tolerance is 10−9 , whereas
the requested gaps range from 10−1 to 10−7 in half-decade steps. Recorded incumbents can lie
as much as 5.5 × 10−9 below the known mathematical optimum because of accepted constraint
violations. Some LP tolerance requests were also refused by SoPlex. These distinctions matter when
comparing the solver with a theorem that assumes valid lower bounds and an exact incumbent.
    The accompanying computational archive preserves the original generation and analysis code,
run records, metadata, selected-instance lists, and numerical summaries. An inventory records
source paths, byte counts, and SHA256 hashes; figure metadata identify every plotted observation
and its original record. The package checks accounting and aggregation without invoking a solver.
Such checks establish the integrity of the retained evidence, rather than the mathematical validity
of floating-point bounds. External MINLPLib model files and solver binaries are not included.

11.2    Tolerance, geometry, and solver representation
The tolerance study contains 20 synthetic instances with optima known by construction. It uses
epigraph formulations min t subject to f (x) ≤ t and the instance constraints, with fixed expression
forms. Each run has a 100,000-node limit and a 120-second limit; a tolerance sequence stops after its
first limit. The setting named model supplies the known optimum as incumbent, disables heuristics,
and uses best-first node selection without plunging. Ordinary propagation remains enabled. These
three changes were made together, so their individual effects are not identified by a comparison
with the default setting.
     The main unconstrained families are built from

       hc (s) = (s − 1)2 (s + 1)2 + c ,       qd (s) = (s + d)4 + (s − d)4 − 12d2 s2 − 2d4 = 2s4 .
                                       


The isolated ison objectives sum hci (xi ), with (c1 , c2 , c3 , c4 ) = (0.5, 0.7, 0.6, 0.8); iso2c adds
0.5(x1 − x2 )4 . The objectives for linediag2 and plane3 are h0.5 (x1 − x2 ) and h0.5 (x1 + x2 − x3 ).

                                                     62
The axis-aligned lineaxis2 adds the unsimplified zero q0.375 (x2 ) − 2x42 to h0.5 (x1 ). The ring2 and
sphere3 objectives are ( i x2i − 1)2 in two and three dimensions. The quartic objectives qflat1,
                            P

qflat2a, qflat2b, and qflat3 respectively use q0.375 (x1 ), h0.5 (x1 ) + q0.375 (x2 ), q0.375 (x1 ) + q0.5 (x2 ),
and h0.5 (x1 ) + q0.375 (x2 ) + q0.5 (x3 ). These expressions are passed in the stated expanded or
unsimplified forms, not replaced by their algebraically simpler identities. The exponential examples
minimize log x2 − x1 subject to x2 ≥ ex1 , or the sum of two such     √ independent copies. The two
                                                           2
McCormick examples minimize 2|d| − d(x2 − b) on [0, 1] , with b = 2 − 1 and d = x1 − 1/3 (aligned)
or d = x1 − x2 − 1/3 (transversal). The archive supplies all coordinate bounds, which are asymmetric
in the non-McCormick examples, and the generated solver formulations.
     Figure 1(a) shows the predicted 1/2 and 1 exponent groups. For the line and sphere families
with quadratic transverse growth, Section 3 predicts power p/2 for a p-dimensional optimal set. The
constrained and McCormick examples additionally depend on their specific gap structure. Under
model, the families with one-dimensional optimal sets have fitted slopes 0.50–0.52, compared with
the predicted exponent 1/2; the families with two-dimensional optimal sets have slopes 1.01–1.05,
compared with 1. The latter fits span only one or two decades; their available sequences cover two
or three decades before the node budget limits further observations. For example, sphere3 has
79, 747, 7,475, and 76,699 processed nodes at tolerances 10−1 through 10−4 . These observations
support the geometric distinction in Section 3, within the tested formulations and search settings.
     Degenerate isolated minima also show why dimension alone is insufficient. For local growth
          qi
   i |ti | , the predicted power is n/2 −
P                                           P
                                              i 1/qi . The quartic examples qflat2b and qflat3 give
fitted powers 0.49–0.51, compared with 1/2. For qflat2a, the predicted power is 1/4 and the model
tail fit is 0.27. The one-dimensional quartic qflat1 has tail and wide fits 0.31 and 0.33 under
model, while controlled bisection gives 0.25 and 0.24. The records do not determine whether the
discrepancy under LP-biased branching persists or reflects the finite tolerance window.
     At isolated nondegenerate minima, model counts grow approximately additively with log(1/ϵ):
the archived fits give 9, 10, 40, and 92 nodes per decade for iso2, iso2c, iso3, and iso4. The
default counts for iso2 and iso3 instead plateau at 95 and 341 nodes. Those runs exhaust their trees
near the numerical feasibility scale. Their plateaus therefore do not establish tolerance-independent
mathematical certificate sizes.
     The archived slopes are least-squares fits of log10 nodes against log10 (1/ϵ). A tail fit uses the
last five available eligible records with ϵ ≤ 10−3 ; a wide fit uses all eligible records with ϵ ≤ 10−2 .
Both require at least three points spanning 0.99 decades. Node-limit, time-limit, and error records
are excluded, whereas exhausted trees remain eligible. A short fit or an exhausted-tree plateau
can therefore differ substantially from an asymptotic exponent. Limits also truncate the harder
sequences, so the slopes describe the observed range rather than an uncensored distribution of solver
work.
     For seven instances satisfying the archived integral-bound hypotheses, recorded final leaves,
including leaves still open at gap termination, were compared with the anisotropic integral lower
bound. No recorded ratio is below one; the smallest is 1.59 on qflat1. Under model, the leaf-to-
bound ratio ranges from 6.84 to 9.78 on iso2, 15.88 to 23.65 on linediag2, and 6.76 to 9.41 on
qflat2a. The last range includes every eligible half-decade observation. This is a consistency check
on recorded leaves and numerically evaluated bounds, not a certified inequality for the floating-point
run.
     Expression representation can change the effective relaxation. On ring2, keeping an outer square
unexpanded gives one processed node at every tested tolerance, whereas model with the expanded
expression uses 37,439 nodes at 10−6 . Shared auxiliary expressions likewise produce one-node runs
on condisk2. Objective-cutoff propagation gives 15 nodes over 10−2 through 10−7 on isofbbt2,
whose fourth-order secant gap lies outside the quadratic gap hypothesis. These examples concern
