Proof of theorem 8.11. Choose π strictly between the two sides of the first inequality in (100). Ridge
regression cannot worsen when a feature is added, so g11,j ≤ gj∗ . Hence π > 0. For an integer block

                                    gj (p) ≥ gj∗ − π(1T p − 1).                                  (197)

Choosing the better feature in each block attains equality. Equality requires exactly one feature per
block. If every ∆j > 0, each block’s better feature is unique, proving uniqueness of the optimum.
    For two different one-per-block supports, let D be the nonempty set of blocks on which they
differ. Their midpoint selects both features with weight one half on D and agrees with one binary

and the binary removal accounting give the claimed leaves and nodes.
    For the stronger relaxation, every point of a block’s original mixed-integer objective epigraph
satisfies the affine inequality tj ≥ gj∗ − π(1T zj − 1), by (197). It remains valid on the closed convex

                                                                           P        P

optimal binary support is feasible for the hull relaxation, so its root value is exactly OPT.

G.4    Hull calculus and deterministic completion
Zero extension maps generators of HT to generators of a larger hull. Moreover, for N ⪰ 0,

                            (z, b, B) ∈ HT      =⇒       (z, b, B + N ) ∈ HT .                   (210)


                                                   107
                                                                   √
For a rank-one N = vv ⊤ , mix (z, b, B) with the generator (1T , v/ t, vv ⊤ /t) with weight t and let
t ↓ 0. The first moments converge to (z, b) and the second moment to B + vv ⊤ . Closedness and
a decomposition of N into rank-one terms prove (210). The singleton hull is 0 ≤ z ≤ 1, B ≥ 0,
b2 ≤ zB. Consequently the global positive semidefinite constraint and the singleton hulls give, for
E = B − ββ ⊤ , E ⪰ 0 and Ejj ≥ βj2 (1/zj − 1). This proves ℓs (z) ≥ gλ (z); integer generators prove
validity. Convexity follows by projecting a convex feasible epigraph.
    For a block T and a positive semidefinite block matrix QT , every generator satisfies b⊤ QT b =
⟨QT , bb⊤ ⟩. Convex combinations and limits imply that the exact closed indicator epigraph hull has
value at most ⟨QT , BT T ⟩ on HT . The residual matrix Q0 ⪰ 0 has ⟨Q0 , B⟩ ≥ β ⊤ Q0 β. Summing
proves the selected-block dominance stated in the text.

Lemma G.3 (A support distribution). For z ∈ K, with any prescribed zero and one fixings, there
is a random feasible binary support with indicator ζ and mean z. For βj = 0 where zj = 0, define
ξj = βj ζj /zj when zj > 0 and zero otherwise. Then Eξ = β and D = Cov ξ satisfies tr D = π(z, β).
On each coordinate subset, (z, β, ββ ⊤ + D) restricts to a point of its local hull.

Proof. Every vertex of K is binary. Indeed, two fractional coordinates can be perturbed in opposite
directions, while a lone fractional coordinate can be perturbed if the budget is slack. If the
budget is tight and only one coordinate is fractional, its value would equal the difference of two
integers, a contradiction. The same argument applies to a face with fixings. Express z as a convex
combination of its binary vertices. The coefficient expectation and variance follow coordinatewise:
Var ξj = βj2 (1/zj − 1). The second-moment points are actual convex combinations of generators.

G.4.1     Completion using zero-indicator helper columns

helper set and assume W = XH XH ⊤ ≻ 0. Define

G.6    Conflict packing and the retained-helper hard theorem
Lemma G.5 (Integer optimum lower bound). Let q = k/n < 1, and let ρ ∈ (q, 1) satisfy
                                                                          !
                 n                                                   p
                   [q log(q/ρ) + (1 − q) log((1 − q)/(1 − ρ))] = log   + log(1/α).
                 2                                                   k

If y is independent of X, then with probability at least 1 − α, OPT ≥ (1 − ρ)∥y∥2 . In the planted
model the same probability bound holds for OPT ≥ (1 − ρ)σ 2 ∥PS⊥∗ w∥2 .

Proof. For a fixed k-subset T , the span of its columns is a uniform k-dimensional subspace. Condi-
tional on an independent nonzero vector v, the squared projection fraction has the law A/(A + C),
where A ∼ χ2k and C ∼ χ2n−k are independent. For ρ > q, exponential Markov applied to
(1 − ρ)A − ρC gives

                    P{A/(A + C) ≥ ρ} ≤ (1 − 2t(1 − ρ))−k/2 (1 + 2tρ)−(n−k)/2 .

Choosing 2t(1 − ρ) = 1 − q/ρ yields the exponential of minus the left side of the displayed equation.
Union-bound over all k-subsets. Ridge values dominate projection residuals, and padding any
smaller support to size k cannot increase its value. This proves the independent case.
    For the planted case, for each T let vT = XS ∗ \T βS∗ ∗ \T + σw. It is independent of XT , and
y − vT lies in its span. Therefore the same fixed-support probability bound applies. Moreover
∥vT ∥ ≥ σ∥PS⊥∗ w∥. The union bound again gives the result. Independence of the different residuals
is unnecessary.

Proof of theorem 9.7. Put α = 1/k. Since k/n → 0 and xp → x, the solution ρ in lemma G.5 satisfies
1 − ρ = e−x + o(1). This follows from q log(q/ρ) + (1 − q) log((1 − q)/(1 − ρ)) = − log(1 − ρ) + o(1),
uniformly on compact subsets of (0, 1). In case (a),

                                       OPT ≥ (e−x − o(1))∥y∥2                                    (215)

with probability tending to one.

                                             B0

    Convexity of a leaf containing two code points forces their midpoint into that leaf, contradicting
its certificate bound. Charging certified removals as additional convex pieces gives at least |C|
terminal or removed pieces. For binaries each node can fix each coordinate at most once, hence
creates at most p such removed pieces; leaves plus removals are at most (p + 1) times the node
count. The lifted construction retains its helper second moments at zero fixings. Deleting those
columns invalidates this feasible point and is not a consequence of the argument.

Equalities between a null absolute correlation and mn have conditional probability zero. Thus the
weak inequality used in the exact convex first-order condition and the strict selected inequality in
the published certificate give the same probability. The displayed random variable is bounded and
converges in probability to the right side of (125), so its expectations converge to that constant. This
calculation uses conditional independence; the unscaled null correlations share a random variance
and need not be unconditionally independent.
    Finally, always R ≤ OPT ≤ Fλ (S). The event R = Fλ (S) implies R = OPT, and on Un the two
events coincide. Hence

                         0 ≤ P{R = OPT} − P{R = Fλ (S)} ≤ P(Unc ) −→ 0.

This proves the global exactness limit without conditioning the Gaussian correlations on an event
involving the null columns.

                                                    116
H.3    The two root exactness probabilities
We prove Proposition 10.2. Put ζi = bTi w. The first-order conditions for u = 0 to minimize the
convex function F on [0, 2]N are ζi ≥ 0 for all i. Conditional on w ̸= 0, these inner products are
independent centered nondegenerate Gaussian variables. Thus their signs are independent fair coins,
proving (132). The matrix has full column rank almost surely, making the objective strictly convex.
A root value equal to the integer optimum therefore forces the unique root minimizer to be an
optimal vertex, and a vertex root minimizer conversely forces value equality.
    Fix a flipped vertex indexed by S, |S| = k, and write z = BS 1 and v = w + 2z. Its box
optimality conditions are bTi v ≥ 0 off S and −bTi v ≥ 0 on S. Given (w, BS ), the N − k off-support
conditions hold with probability 2−(N −k) . Summing the conditions on S gives the necessary condition
−z T w − 2∥z∥2 ≥ 0. Here z ∼ N (0, a2 IM ) independently of w, with a2 = ρk/N . For λ > 0 in the
range where the moment exists,
                                   T            2
                                                                              −M/2
                            Eeλ(−z w−2∥z∥ ) = 1 + 4λa2 − λ2 a2                         .

At λ = 2, Markov’s inequality bounds the necessary event by (1 + 4ρk/N )−M/2 . Its indicator is
measurable with respect to (w, BS ), so multiplication by the conditional off-support probability is
valid. Summing over vertices gives the first inequality in (133); for k = 0 use the already proved
probability 2−N . Finally, log(1 + 4ρk/N ) ≥ (k/N ) log(1 + 4ρ) by concavity. The binomial theorem
then gives the second inequality.

transfers their joint distribution to the event S = S. On that event the fitted vector is Cu◦ = −PLS v
and the residual is (I − PLS )v. Thus the same joint law holds for X, Z conditional on the selected
support. Supports of the same size have identical laws, proving the claimed mixture and (138) by
Lemma H.1.
    This proof transfers sign-invariant quantities from a fixed support. It does not treat the signed
regression vector as independent of the event selecting its support.

H.7    An entropy bound for overlapping supports
Lemma H.3 (Overlap forces an entropy loss). Let F be a nonempty family of k-subsets of an
n-set, where 1 ≤ k < n, and let pi be the fraction of its members containing i. Suppose 0 < λ ≤ 1,
P 2                     2
  i pi ≥ λk, and λ/2 ≥ e k/n. Then

                                             n                        √
                                             k        λk     n
                                 log              ≥      log    − log(8 k).                    (228)
                                         |F|           2     ek

Proof. Let S be uniform on F, represented by its indicator vector. Entropy subadditivity gives
log |F| ≤ i h(pi ), where h(p) = −p log p − (1 − p) log(1 − p), with 0 log 0 = 0. Indeed, the entropy
          P

chain rule writes the joint entropy as a sum of conditional entropies, and conditioning cannot
                               P
increase entropy. For q = k/n, i pi = k implies
                                     X                             X
                                             h(pi ) = nh(q) −          d(pi ∥q),
                                     i                             i

where d(p∥q) = p log(p/q) + (1 − p) log((1 − p)/(1 − q)). Using log x ≥ 1 − 1/x on the second term
gives
                             d(p∥q) ≥ ϕ(p) := p log(p/q) + q − p ≥ 0.


                                                         120
The last inequality follows since ϕ is convex with minimum zero at p = q. Put L = log(1/(eq)) ≥ 1.
We claim ϕ(p) ≥ Lp(p − λ/2) for 0 ≤ p ≤ 1. For p ≤ λ/2 this follows from nonnegativity. For
p ≥ λ/2 it suffices that log(p/q) − 1 − L(p − λ/2) ≥ 0. This function is concave; at p = λ/2 it equals
log(λ/(2q)) − 1 ≥ 1, and at p = 1 it equals Lλ/2 ≥ 0. The claim follows throughout the interval.
Summing gives                                                  !
                              X                X
                                                   2   λX           Lλk
                                 d(pi ∥q) ≥ L     pi −       pi ≥        .
                               i                i
                                                        2 i          2
Thus log |F| ≤ nh(q) − Lλk/2.
    We also need a lower estimate for nk . For V ∼ Bin(n, q), k = nq is a mode, as follows by taking
                                        

                                      √ Var(V ) = nq(1 − q) ≤ k, Chebyshev’s
ratios of consecutive probabilities. Since                                  √     inequality places
probability at least 1/2 on |V − k| < 2k. This interval contains at most 4 k integers, each with
probability at most P{V = k}. Therefore
                                  !

Taking logarithms gives log nk ≥ nh(q) − log(8 k). Combining the estimates proves (228).

H.8    The convex-piece lower bound
We prove (150) in precisely the two regimes used in Theorem 10.8: divergent ρN = o(N ), or fixed
sufficiently large ρ. Constants here are intentionally conservative, allowing an elementary operator
estimate. Set

The quantity λ log ρ is bounded for ρ ≥ ρ0 . Since k ≤ N , the two negative terms are at most
log(8N ) for all sufficiently large N . This proves (150).
    The proof covers arbitrary convex pieces, because it uses only a class’s vertices and their
barycenter. It does not use a prescribed variable order, a branching rule, or an assumption that
the planted vertex is optimal. The value comparison (234) is what connects the planted descent
directions to the target at the actual integer optimum.
